THE WILD WEST SHOW AT WARRINGTON. Animated Scenes on Wilderspool Causeway.

When Warringtonians awoke on Sunday morning, Col. W. F. Cody, known far and wide as Buffalo Bill, had -pitched his tents in a field adjoining Wilderspool Cause- way. Naturally, the visit of such a world-famed exhibition as the Wild West had aroused the interest of thetownspeople to the highest pitch and during the day thousands gratified their desire to acquaint themselves 'with the mode of life of the Indian tribes and repre- sentives of other races who constitute the followers of the famous scout. All roads . seemed to lead to Wilderspool, and towards the evening the Cause- way was well nigh impassable. The weather was glorious and the scene was one of animation. On Monday the atmospheric conditions were again all that could be desired and from the forenoon until evening the thoroughfares leading to the ground were thronged. Many workpeople enjoyed a holiday and the Corpora- tion reaped a rich copper harvest as the result of the enormous traffic by the trams. There were two per- formances—the first in the afternoon and the other in the evening—and both were remarkably well-attended the one in the evening probably creating a record for Warrington in this direction. The show itself wasab- solutely unique. It was not given in the type of :marquee -usually erected by the proprietors of travelling exhibitions. The vast arena was open to the sky but the seats allotted to the spectators were canopied so as to afford complete protection against any vagaries of the climate. When the doors opened to admit the public to the evening performance there was a rush for tickets such as has perhaps never before been witnessed in Warrington. The pay boxes were literally beseiged and the task which fell to the lot of the men in charge was by no means an enviable one. Most of the seats were speedily occupied and the commencement of the performance was eagerly awaited. Of the exhibition it may truthfully be said that it entirely came up to expectations. There was a fine Yankee flavour about the expeditious manner in which the programme was carried through. There was hardly a moment's pause from beginning to end and everything passed off without the slightest hitch. The openiug scene was one calculated to leave a lasting impression on the mind. Rough riders from nearly every quarter of the globe— the" Indians in all the glory of war paint and feathers— entered the arena on galloping steeds. All formed into line and then the veteran Col. Cody, the cynosure of all eyes, entered to receive a flattering ovation. In a few words the Colonel introduced his 'Congress of the rough riders of the world" and then to the accompani- ment of sprightly music the horsemen commenced to execute a series of movements almost bewildering in their intricacy yet carried out with an ease and pre- cision which evoked the warmest admiration. As the living wealth of colour passed before the eyes of the spectators the spectacle was one which formed a fitting prelude to the evening's enjoyment. Horsemanship is, of course, the one great feature of the Wild West exhibition. The public were held spell-bound by the feats of the rough riders whose wonderful agility and daring were quite a revelation to many. The Cossacks especially excelled in this department. Their intrepidity seemed unbounded, in fact they could ride their horses bare-backed in almost any position. If only to see the splendid specimens of horseflesh a visit to the show was well repaid. The animals were in the pink of condition, and the smart grooming was evidence of the careful attention bestowed upon them. Space forbid* reference to each item of the programme but some demand special mention. Johnnie Baker,the celebrated young American marksman, gave a rema.tkably clever exhibition, having no difficulty in hitting balls thrown into the air whilst lying down or standing on his head. The use of the lasso was interestingly illustra- ted by a group of Mexicans, and a number of Arabs proved themselves experts in acrobatic feats. A singularly realistic spectacle was the battle of San Juan Hill, the troops introduced including a detachment from Roosevelt's Rough Riders who dis- tinguished themselves in the actual engagement. The first scene was the bivouac before the kattle and the frolics of .the soldiers around the camp fires were watched with interest and pleasure. Then fol- lowed the storming of the hill which was carried out with fine effect several big guns as well as the small firearms being brought into requisition. The "fun" in which the daring Cowboys indulged was really astonish- ing. Whilst ridingat full gallop they picked uparticles from the ground and wild horses were easily lassoed. The performance which proved the most interesting as well as exciting was the riding of the bucking bronchos. These inherently vicious animals strenuously resisted all efforts to control them, but when once the cowboys had mounted they could not be shaken off. A tribe of Indians delighted the audience with war dances and there weres mart military exercises by veteran English and American cavalrymen. Another noteworthy item was the attack on the old Deadwood coach by Indians

E*

who were, however, repulsed by Buffalo Bill and his attendant cowboys. Colonel Cody demonstrated his renowned ability as a marksman, all his clever feats of shooting being performed on horseback. • A salute by the entire congress of rough riders concluded the enter- tainment, and shortly after midnight "Buffalo Bill's Wild West" was speeding on its way to Birkenhead where on Tuesday the events which had delighted thou- sands of Warringtonians on the previous day were re-enacted.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
AT RIIYL.

Thousands of people crowded into Rhyl on Wednes- day, where Buffalo Bill's monster organization gave two performances. The town was packed as if in the height of the season, over 15,000 excursionists arriving by 'road and rail.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S CHAT.

The London season has this year received a great im- pptus from the presence of Royalty on many public occasions. During the past few weeks their Majesties the King and Queen have fulfilled many engagements as have also the Prince and Princess of Wales, and other members of the Royal Family. Some rest from public engagements is afforied by Epsom week, and the Whit- suntide recess, during which the Court will remain at Windsor Castle.

The Prince and Princess of Wales, and their children, will also be mostly at Windsor, staying at Frpgmore House until after Ascot race week, which begins on Monday June 15. The arrangements for the expected visits from the King of Italy and the President of the French Republic, are as yet unsettled, but they will not take place until July. after which the Royal visit to Ireland will be the principal event in their Majesties engagements before the autumn holidays. The garden party which the Prince and Princess of Wales gave at Marlborough House to the Presidents, Lady Presidents, and members of the League of Mercy, could, not have been held under better conditions Per- fect weather favoured the wearing of summer toilettes, whilst the gardens with their fresh lawns and wealth of new foliage, were at their best. About fifteen hundred members of the League were the guests of the Prince, who presented decorations to some of its principal workers and promoters. The League was formed in 1899 for the purpose of raising money for King Edward's Hospital Fund, and it has already handed over a sub- etantial sum for the benefit of the London hospitals. The League enjoys the patronage of the King, and the Royal hospitality at Marlborough House will help to enhance the charitable enthusiasm of its members.

One of the great Charity Fetes of the season will be a matinee concert for the benefit of the League of Mercy which has been arranged for June 5, at Grosvenor House, the residence of the Duke and Duchess of West- minster. This will be under the immediate patronage of the Princess of Wales and the Duchess of Albany, both of whom are Lady Presidents of the League. The other principal charity social gathering of the season, will be the ball at the Albert Hall on June 9th, in which the Countess of Derby is the moving spirit. The great London hostesses have not organized a bazaar this year, but they will give'all their assistance to the ball, both of which are designed for the benefit of the London hospitals. As a rule the weather at Whitsuntide is not very genial or settled, but the recent burst of sunshine and warmth came just in time to cheer the hearts of those London tradespeople who havean aristocratic clientigle. The change was very badly wanted, as the continued cold weather kept people indoors, and no one is disposed to order dainty fresh garments whilst compelled to sit by a fire. The milliners, dressmakers and others were complaining that in spite of a good deal of entertaining amongst the higher ranks of Society, the weather since Easter has been sadly detrimental to business and com- mercial interests, generally. It is scarcely possible that the loss can be wholly made up in the now shortened , season, and many tradespeople are complaining that ' business is falling off from economic reasons. A very general complaint amongst milliners, dress- makers, jewellers, and silversmiths is that customers who used to come to them, now spend their money on motor cars, and another class of shopkeepers complain that a great many people who used to entertain at home now give their dinner and supper parties at the hotels and restaurants.

The modish black ostrich feather gains steadily in popularity, and cannot be too long or too thick. That we are also to have the coloured ostrich plume with us this summer is a certainty, huge shaded specimens appearing on much of the newest headgear. or many years past milliners have left coloured ostrich feathers severely alone' but those now offered us—some shading from the palest yellow to the deepest orange, from soft pia to dark tones of red: from lily leaf green to brightest emerald, are calculated to make one regret their long banishment. Veils to match the predominating colour in the hat or toque, are being supplied by leading milliners, the effect in many cases leaving much to be desired. In brown, blue, and even some shades of green, the idea does not often lead to disastrous results, but when such colours as red, yellow, and violet are wm, the wearers appear to be possessed of the most horrible complexion possible. Why the fashionable woman should permit herself -0 be disfigured—for the time being—in this particular way, is a problem difficult of solution, but the fact remains that she does. Nothing looks so well in veils as a fine cobwebby black one, and to this, if a woman is wise, she will, despite all novelties, remain faithful.

Much has been said about the revival of book mus- lin for which the Paris and Vienna modistes have pre- dicted a great vogue this summer.- It is true that the old-fashioned book muslin is being employed for many an airy fairy gown, but so little of the muslin is seen when the dressmakers have finished with it, that it would appear immaterial what fabric is used as a foundation for the wealth of embroideries and inser- tions lavished upon it. A greater contrast to The old world book muslin gown, with its simple soft fichu and sashand utter absence of all ornamentation could scarcely be imagined. One would hardly think that the trying occupation of young women who serve in shops, was -conducive to their taking up the walking craze which was started by the Stock Exchange competition. Yet it seems an at- tempt has been made to organise a race between the girls who serve in the shops of two of the tea com- panies which supply London with this kind of refresh- ment. The value of walking as an exercise for both men and women cannot be overestimated, but pedestrian feats which are accomplished for wagers are not de- sirable things for women to go in for,least of all through the streets of London as proposed by the tea girls. One of the disadvantages of emigration is generally considered to be the difficulty in obtaining the assist- ance of a dentist, but no doubt in some way or other settlers in a new country manage to supply the de- ficiency. In any event they would be in as favorable a position as the majority of inhabitants of Burnham Thorpe in Norfolk, which is famous as the birthplace of Nelson. There, it is said, the village blacksmith• is still the only dentist. We are not told what instrument he uses, but whatever his skill may be. the fact—if it is a fact—affords something of lux anachronism in this 20th century. •

AN ABSCONDING TENANT,

On Monday at the Runcorn Petty Sessions Robert Bond was summoned at the instance of John Poole for having removed furniture from 23, Cavendish street, of which he was the former tenant to avoid payment of rent, £9.65. owing from the 27th February 1903.—The evidence showed that Bond had gone to Wrexham to avoid paytnent of the rent after having removed and sold his furniture. He had given his former landlord a great deal of trouble and after promising to pay had failed to keep his word. An order of 10s. monthly was made against the defendant.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

Delicate coloured materials of the most fascinating description will be much worn this summer, Perhaps the most popular is a misty greyish blue much resembling the colour of the heavens on a hot summer day when the blue sky is half veiled by a soft mist, This' charming colour is termed zenith in Paris, and will be found generally becom- ing, but more especially so to fair skins and blonde hair, although, indeed, the colour of the hair of the wearer is not so important as the clearness of the complexion. Light tones are undoubtedly more in vogue than sombre colours, and delicate reseda, pale heliotrope, champignon, and silver grey are among the principal favourites. Canvas and voile cos- tumes are at present in the ascendant trimmed with cgarse woollen laces and embroideries, their smartness emphasized by a liberal amount of strappings and tuckings. In fact, it is really impossible to find a dress which is not adorned with lace or embroidery in form cr another.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
'OULARD TOILETTE!

The wardrobe of a well-dressed woman this season will not be complete without a foulard dress, as for morning wear it is just. the thing, and the addition of a dainty lace cape or ruffle will tarn it into an afternoon cos- tume de visite. Foulard is so delightfully soft that it is particularly adapted to the plissé skirt, and many of the prettiest gowns of this material are made in this style. The costume sketched is of foulard trimmed with lace, although • silk insertion could be substituted for the lace if preferred. The skirt is beautifully tucked and the whole toilette is quite in the latest mode. Some very pretty foulards are shot with two or three colours and spotted with irregular groups of black or white spots, while others have in addition to this a delicate check or tartan pattern as well. Grass lawn is another material which will be much and well worm this season, and one of the prettiest French model gowns in grass lawn was trimmed with pale green 'figured foulard outlined with lace guipure. The effect was very good indeed. The new grass lawns are very thin and silky, and some are openworked all over with small holes. There are some very charming cape-

A DAINTY BLOUSEI

lines for wear during the summer months; which have for trimming a lace scarf with green leaves embroidered or appliqued into the material found the brim:

Tussore silks made with insertions of plissé lace vandykes at the hem, and with lace medallions scattered at regular intervals over the material itself make up into exceedingly elegant toilettes which are cool to look at and to wear, and are also very durable. This material does not crush or soil easily and is therefore one to meet with universal approval. The dainty blouse given in my second sketch is made of washing lace trimmed with lace insertion, and is of a soft green colour, contrasting pleasantly with the pale coffee tint of the lace. The sleeves which are tucked to the elbow, are full round the wristd The chief feature of the newest blouses seems to be the elaboration of the sleeves, which are very full and pendulous below the elbow and ornamented •Nvith tuckings, gaugings or shaped pieces of lace above. • Sometimes shaped frills take the place of the fulness at the elbow, the under sleeve in such cases con- sisting of a tight gauntlet of lace, which con- tinues from just below the elbow to the wristd The blouse is still one of the most honoured items of dress, but must nowadays be chosen carefully so as to harmonise, not only with the costume, but with the circumstances under which it will be worn. Unless it is of the lingerie type, it must suit in every detail with the rest of ihe dress. There are some very pretty batiste slips which are finely embroidered, the favourite being a black and white batiste encrusted with coarse ecru laced

PRETTY HAT.

Also popular for morning wear are those ox tussore silk or toile of a holland tint worked with black, and finished at the throat by a silk cravat and smart coquettish bow. A smart style show,s a blonse boxpleated down tile front alternated with bands of lace, in- sertion or embroidery, and an interlaced cravat down the centr:.-i. Another mock: per- tains more to the bolero shape, and has a deep cape - like . collar and folded shaped waistband. •

The pretty hat sketched is of fine white straw, and has a sweeping feather fall on the hair behind. It is eminently chic. It is in the putting on of the newest hats that the most successful effects are arrived at, and some which are in reality absolutely fiat as to crown and trimming, manage to give the effect of height by the sharp turning back of the brim from the face or at the sides. The Directoire style of hat may be worn with or without strings, as suits the individual fancy, while the rounded poke brim is very becoming to a face in the first bloom of youth, but looks rather ridiculous on any but the ingenue. There is a very charming piece of headgear which has recently been evolved from the clever brain of one of our smartest milliners, and which is a sort of glorified sunbonnet in frilled muslin or zephyrs with a little cachepigne of lace at the back to protost the neck from sunburn. • Cogturrraf

Before she could • reply, Grant entored, and having overheard the last question was the first to speak. "If you are talking of Mr. Pillage," ha aaid, "ho is not in prison, or is Mr. Vane. They Al?. both at the Nelson Hotel, but are being watched. As soon as Mr. Will's ease is dismissed, one or the other will be arrested."

"How do you mean one or the other, Grant?" "Why, Mr. Pillage says that Vane is guilty, and Mrs. Bedowen takes her Bible oath that Mr. Pil- lage strangled the deceased." Grant was fond of long words. "I expect as Mrs. Bedowen's accusa- tion is the most definite that Mr. Pillage will be arrested."

"And who will believe her?" demanded Miss Jinney, scornfully. "No one who know her, certainly," replied Gkrant, in his best English. "She's a bad lot. Do you remember when she came on the tramp here five years ago, Miss? We saw her, you and L" _ _ _ _

"Yes: And I held my toaague when she became housekeeper to Mr. Tremewan. I wish I hadn't now. If I had told him, of her plight, he might have hesitated to engage her." Oh, no the wouldn't" answered Grant, "there is some secret she knew about Mr. Tremewan that made him her slave. I don't know what it is exactly," he added, scratching his head, "I can't find out."

"If you did it wouldn't do mach good to the Captain," said Tom, with a gloomy air; "but I know exactly how to act." "So do I," replied Miss Jinney, with a glance at Tom.

"What do you mean?" asked .Grant, curiously. Tom never let on that he heard the question. "Where is that devil of a woman?" he demanded. "Over at Salcombe."

"Ho ! They never watcthed her." "Oh, no. There is no need to. She is quite ready to (mile forward, and give her evidence when called upon." "She's a liar, growled Tom fiercely, and struck his fist on the table. "See here Grant, and you, Miss. I met the Captain in South America, and he saved say life. Would a man who saved one life take another? No! I don't believe he killed Mr. Tremewan. lam a native of these parts, and now that I know the Captain is a Pillage I am mcfrb than ever bound to help him. My people were his people's tenants for years and years. But the Captain never told me who he was. "He is Walter Pillage," said Miss Jinney, fold- ing her arms, "I have good reason to know. At one time " she checked herself with some emotion. " Ab., well," said she‘with a sigh, "those days are pest. But tor the sake of them I hope you and I will save him, Tam." "Can you?" asked Grant, anxiously. Then seeing assent on both faces, he continued. " Per- haps I may "You shut up and mind your own business," growled the sailor; "this lady and I know what to do. I'm going to make Mrs. Beclowen tell the truth—if 1 choke her in getting it out." "She has told the truth."

. "So she says," replied Miss Jimmy, with a sneer. " lies, Grant, just to feather her own nest. Where are you going, Tam?" The sailor paused at the door. "On business connected with this damnable business," he said, determinedly. "I'm going to save Mr. Pillage— Captain Max, that was. I'll come back soon." Let me help you!" pried Miss Jinney eagerly, and jumped up. have a right to help." "You shall," said Tom, with an oath, and he clapped her on the shoulder. Many a woman would have been knocked down by such a smack, but Miss Jinney stood it bravely, and 'vas rather gratified by receiving it. "As to you, Grant. You mind your own business or I'll smasb you," and in his turn Torn went to see In- spector Tait. It was perfectly true that Pillage was at the Nelson Hotel. Vane was there also, but the two having done with one another, kept to their separate rooms. Beyond the police authorities no one knew of the accusations, nor that the two men were being watched. Pillage had *plenty of money, and told the landlord he would remain fr Darthridge till after the trial of Will Hannaforc: at Exeter. The landlord saw nothing extra ordinary in this, as he fancied that Pillage—of rather that Captain Max, for the name was still retained—was interested in the matter as one of the pilic. Had he known the truth he might have made some difficulty about his guest remain- ing, but knowing nothing, he was delighted to have a new boarder. Thus it came about that when Mrs. Tremewan came to call on Captain Max, she was shown up to his sitting-coons without delay. In spite of Dora,'s objections, Mrs. Tremewan had insisted on seeing her husband, as she wished to know what he intended to do. The man was glad to see her, and received her with an evident sense of relief. In fact, he seemed to have expected her. "And if you hadn't come I should have sent for you," he declared. Mrs. Tremewan was surprised by this greeting. "I have nothing now," she said bitterly, "that is nothing to make you wish me to come back to you as your wife. Paul has the property now." Thanks to my appearance,' replied Pillage, calmly; "but I have a more important thing to say to you than that Amy. Will you sit down?" Mrs. Tremewan took the seat he pointed out. She looked much older than formerly, and her eyes were red with weeping. Pillage gave her a glance of conaniseration' and went to see that the door was closed. He also examined the windows.

"Why do you do that?" asked his wife. , • "Because 1 am about to tell you something," he answered, sitting- down. "bee here, Amy, do many people know of this businec-s?" "Only inspector Tait and the police autho- trities," she replied in a low voice. "If the matter could stop here, there would be no scandal. I fear, however, that at your trial "What! Do you believe in the lies of Mrs. Beelowen?" he demanded. _

Mrs. Treinewan sighed. "I don't know what to believe," she said; "of course it is very shock- ing that I should call on a an= who killed my irus-that is Francis-. But, perhaps, you did not kill him."

"I certainly. did not," said Pillage, emphati- cally; "this is the revenge of that woman Bedowen. Now lam about to have my revenge— that is—" he added sihrumoin, his shoulders, "1 am going to tell you something she would rather were not known. But as it will trouble her very little I am wrong in calling it a revenge." "What is it, Walter?" Pillage rose and began to walk up and down the room. 'He was very uneasy, and evaded his wife's eyes. "Tell me, Amy," he asked in an abrupt tone, "why did you come here?" . - "To ask what is to be done," replied Mrs. Tre- mewan, in accents of positive despair. "I am your wife after all. I want to arrange something so that I can leave this place with Dora. Ido not want a scandal—it would kill me. I came to ask you if you think that all this family business will come oat in court."

"Which is as much as to ask iii am to be tried for my life," said Pillage, in his sarcastic way. "I really can't say. I can only deny that 1 li:tiled Trpmewan, but I have no evidence- to prove my innocence."

"Could not Vane?" "He might. But you see I have accused him of the (rime. If he did not kill Tremewan I non% know who did At tall events, whether he is in- nocent or guilty, Vane must have been hovering round the house at the time ot the murder. He certainly might be able to prove my innocence. Unless a miracle happens, Amy, I think I really shall be up on my trial. I may be hanged—l eel- &linty shall be if Mrs. Bediowen can manage it." "Why does she hate you so, Walter?" " Ah!" replied Pillage, bitterly, "that is one of my youthful sins coining home to me. Amy," he paused in his walk and stopped. before her, "I have treated you badly. 1 have thought only of myself. But at one time I really loved you." Yes. I know that. But now—" Mrs. Tre- mewan made a gesture of despair. "All that sort of thing is over," he assented. "Vane will allow you an income, and I shall dis- appear. I shall, probably, go back to south America, where I have friends. Thus all things will be forgotten. I desire you to forget me. have been a bad man, and I must pay for my bad- ness."

Pillage did this sort of thing very, well. He really neither felt nor cared for the misery he had brought on Mrs. Tremewan, but it suited his pre- sent mood to be sentimental. He was about to tell her how thoroughly bad he was, and so pre- pared the way by trying to touch her heart. But Mrs. Tremewa.n's heart was not so easily touched. "You have been a. bad man," she said coldly, "and you are still. The only reparation you can make is to leave England before you are arrested, and never came into mtv life wain. By

danger ot taus miserable story being made public will pass away." "You want me to bolt—in plain English?" " Ye,t, I do. I think you owe that much to me. Paul will allow me a certain income although not what I have been accustomed to. Dora will marry- young Hannaford, and so things will be put. right." "But any feelings," began Pillage. • He was stop- ped by the look of disgust on the face of his wile, and burst out laughing. "You are right, Amy," he said, "all that sort of thing is at an end. "I can't defend myself against the charge of Mrs. Bedowen although I a,m perfectly; innocent. The story I told you is the truth. If Vane did not kill Tremewan I know of no one else who had any reason to do so. I will run away to save your feelings and my own neck. I can clear out in my yacht. She is at Salcombe, and once on board I'll soon get away. TOM can arrange it all, and I do not think there will be much difficulty in baffling that thick-headed idiot Inspector Tait. But before 1 go, Amy, just to soften your feelings towards me

"They oan never be softened," said Mrs. Tre- mewan, violently. "Well, perhaps not. However, I am going to leave behind me a fortune for you Amy. I am sure you deserve it." "I will have none of your money, Walter." "Be calm, Amy. There is no chance of your getting any. I have hardy enough for myself. The fortune I leave to you is that of your late hus- band, Francis Tremewan. I am sorry for Vane, but

Mrs. Tremewan was on her feet, staring incredu- lously. "What folly is this," she said, harshly, "you are my husband." "Indeed lam not. I married you in London, it is true. But I was not free to marry." "You were not free?" Muttered Mrs. Tremewan, starting back. "No. My real wife, who is still alive,—whom I married before you became the second Mrs. Pil- lage,—is Judith Warton, better known to you as Mrs. Bedowen. Yes. She is my wife." Mrs. Tremewan sank on the sofa pale and trem- bling." Oh, you wicked—wicked----" she began until checked by a burst' of tears.

CHAPTER XXIII.

A STARTLING COMMUNICATION.

" Sue 1" said the Colonel in his brusque blatant wanner at breakfast, "you generally know all that is going on at The Gray House. What is the matter now?" "What do you mean, Colonel?" asked his wife before her daughter could speak; "there is nothing wrong, I trust." "If you will wait till I have Sue's answer," cried Gtillard, fiercely, t' there will be some chance of our being enlightened. Don't be a .complete fool, madam. Talk! .Talk! Talk! Speak up, Sue."

"It is something about the death of Mr. Tre- mewan, I think," replied Sue, when Mrs. (Allard had subsided behind the tea-Urn. "Dora will not tell me the facts. She says she is not permitted." " ll'm ! That sounds as though something was very wrong. The murder! I thought that was i.ll settled, and that Will Hannaford Was to be hanged. He ought to be; he killed that poor wretch, Tremewan." "Colonel! Colonel !" said Mrs. Gillard, "the boys!" "The boys !" said the domestic tyrant with a glare at the ourious .faces of his sons. "Let nic hear a wand out of their mouths, and I shall stop their dinner. Always eating—always guzzling. Is that the way to bring up a family, Mrs. Gil- lard ?"

"No," replied the p >or woman, meekly, and very sorry she had spoken. "Then don't let me (hear any nonsense about the boys, madam." "Father," said Tom the eldest hope of the house. "I know something." "Then why didn't: you say so before? No brains. What is it? Don't shuffle. Out with it! Now, sir," and the Colonel glared again. "I heard it from a policemen," said Tom hurriedly swallowing the food that was in his mouth, and nearly choking in the attempt; "he toad &Tie that two other chaps are accused of the murder."

"Confound it," gobbled the Colonel, growing purple, "do you mean to say that young Hanna- ord is innocent?"

"I never thought he was anything else," put in Sue' valiantly. "You, Miss? What do you know of such things? Stick to your needle, and cooking. Who are the other men, Tom?" "One is called Vane, and

." Vane? God bless me, that must .be the cousin. A silly, feather-headed idiot, a simpering jackass. He kill a man?—he couldn't kill a fly! He's inhocent," said Gillard, decisively, "and the other. Speak up !" "Pillage, I think the name is."

" PillageL-Walter Pillage," roared the Colonel, with a snort. " Pooh ! He is dead long ago—long Kt). Don't tell lies, my boy." "I'm only telling you what the policeman told me," said Tom, doggedly. "This Pillage accuses Vane, and Mrs. Bedowen "That infernal woman! Well?"

"She -accuses Pillage," continued Tom, quickly. " Pil.a,re bus been stopping at Seloombe under the nameof Oaptain Max." "Ho ! And the policeanian told. you all this did he—told a boy like you that can't hold his tongue "You made me speak, father," said the sulky Tom.

"And why shouldn't I? As your father I have a right to know everything, by George. But it's this policeman. He ought to be drummed out of the tome fOT betraying official secrets. Who is he r

" I forget his name," %lid Tom, alarmed for the friendly constable.

"NO evasion, sir. Tell me his name at once. I'll speak to Tait about the matter. Out with it."

'ATM glared. He had as nice a temper as his reopeoted father in his own juvenile way, and was determined not to give away (his friend. "I don't know," he growled, and shut his mouth, obsti- nately.

Strange to say the Colonel did not press him further. He felt, that the boy was right, and should not betray a friend, even if that friend were only a constable. But he relieved his mind in another way by attacking his wife. "A nice way you bring up your children, madam !" he snorteu. " Leave the table Tom; I'll. deal with you later. Wait! Do you know any more?" "No, sir," said Tom, and departed with a crust of bread. He knew well that his father would ask him no more questions. " You are hard on the. boy, Colonel," said Mrs. Gillard.

"By George, madam, if I wasn't hard the whole lot of the boys would be in gaol. They are all ripeninc, for the gallows, thanks to the training they 'have had. This coffee is beastly—all grounds. I shall drink no more. Poison, madam, poison !" "Shall I make some more, father?" asked Sue, calmly. "No. You make even worse coffee than your mother. Pillage, eh? Do you remember Pillage, Sophia?" with a glare at his wife. "To be sure 1 do," said Mrs. Gillard, in a flutter; "be was a handsome young man in love with Mrs. Tremewan—before she married the into Mr. Tremewan, of course." "I remember. And Hanna,ford was the third fool; Hump! It is queer that Pillage should return to be mixed up in this crime. And Hanna- ford's son also in it: I wonder what it all, nmans? Egad, I'll see Tait at once. -Here is something in- teresting. Finished my breakfast, madam? Of course, I have, and I feel sick—deadly sick. If you can't provide better meals I shall dine out," and he marched away. There was a sigh of relief when he departed. Tom come back, and the poor mother enjoyed her- self greatly, as she always did when alone with her children. As for the Colonel, he went to se In- spector Tait. So much for the Gillard household. But a dis- cussion was also going on at The Eight Bells be- tween Tom and Miss Jinney. The former had come over from Salcombe finding that his master did not return to the yacht at the appointed time, He called at several places in the hope of finding Captain Max—for by that name only did Toni know him—but not finding him he had repaired to Miss Jinney for information. She, also, was de- voted to the Captain; and she, if anyone, would know of his whereabouts

And Miss Jinney did know'. Thanks to Grant, who was a friend of hers, and who, employed by the liannafords, knew a great deal of what was taking place, she was in a position to advise Tom as to what had happened te his master. The tale was rather garbled in the telling, for what Grant had not known he had either invented or guessed. But Miss .Jinney had the gist-of the matter correct enough, and was as enraged ,aS Tom over the pre- sent position of affairs. "She's a bad one that Mrs. Bedowen," said the gigantic landlady. "To thinlc.she should denounce Mr. Pillage in this way !" "You mean Captain Max," growled Tom. "I call him by the name I know him best by," retorted Miss Jinney, with asperity. "Now that the truth is out, I may as well give him his.proper name. The Pillage family were very well known hereabouts, Tom. They have an old seat on the moors—a fine old place. Though to be sure it has passed out of their hands. Walter Pillage is the last of the family. And a pretty pass he has come t 0.,,

" How does he stand now?" demanded the man, tfhoughtfully. "He is being detained on suspicion of being con- cerned in the murder of Mr. Tremei,van. From what Grant told me, I gathered that Mr. Pillage accuses Mr. Vane of the crime, and Mrs. Bedowen says the Captain is guilty. I don't know much about the law," added Miss Jinney, rubbing her nose with a vexed air, "but it seems that two people can't be accused of the same crime at the same time unless they are what they call accom- plices. Mr. Will is going to be tried for his life, and until his case is settled, neither Mr. Vane nor Mr. 4Piillage can be arrested. But they can be detained, and they are detained." "Are they in prison?" asked Tom,. wrathfully.

OF THE GREAT

IRE HYDE PARR DEMONSTRATION,

On Saturday afternoon, and on part of Saturday evening Hyde Park was occupied by some quarter of a million people who were actively or passively demonstrating against the London Education Bill, s The crowd began to stream in at the Apsley Gate before five c'clock, and it was still coming in after six. It had come in from the Embank- ment, where it began to assemble before four in the afternoon. It had come from all parts of London, and it was drawn up in a double line for countermarching, stretching from Charing Cross Bridge to Blacicfriars. At the head of the procession, after its five mounted Police and its marshal, marched the Thames Barge Builders and the London Society of Compositors. There were almost as many women and children—who came in vehicles— as men, and no one could deny the procession, With its orderly, cheery detachments of tens of thousands strong, and its bands and flying banners, the attribute of imposingness. In the park it was, as it arrived, drawn up in a crescent, with twelve platforms. For an hour Speaker after speaker harangued the people, threatened the Government, and denounced the London Education Bill. The procession will not be remembered for its scores of bands or its hundreds of banners and flags, but for its tens of thousands of earnest faces and its fervent songs. Their songs, led hy the bands or by chapel choirs, breathed the spirit of stead- fast Nonconformity, From thousands of throats there went up as the procession toiled to the park such well-known rallying hymns as " The Church's One Foundatio, "Onward Christian Soldiers," "Sound the battle-cry!" "Fonvcrd be our watchword," and "Men of England, rally!" Two great features of the procession were the absence of children and the presence, in their thousands, of ladies, many of whom were fashionably dressed. Most of the ladies rode in brakes or other vehicles, but there were many who tramped the whole distance in the heat from such remote districts IS Rotherhithe, Limehouse, Dalston, and Woolwich. Everyone *ore a blue favour—a ribbon, a rosette, a medallion, a bunch of forget-me-nots or of bluebells. Even before the procession arrived there was a gathering of sympathetic spectators large enough to flake a decent demonstration; and the hawk- were reaping a fine harves.. They were there by the thousand, selling programmes, totivenir handkerchiefs and slashing pamphlets Against the Bill. Some sold funeral cards. 'Ere y'are. On'y a penny. Gov'ment immorial- cards. Sad deff o' the Eddikveshun 13ill." Trade was stimulated by a black coffin to!aring the remains of the Bill, with a skull hid cross-bones for blazonry.

MAX OrRELL.

Paul Blouet, better known as Max O'Rell, diel on Sunday evening last, was fifty- years of age. He was a native of Brittany, where he was born on March znd, 1848, and was educat;...d at Paris. His college days over. he entered the French army, receiving a cav- alry commission in 1870, just before the out- break of the war with Prussia. He served through the Franco- Prussian cam- and was among those who surrendered at Sedan. After his return to Paris he was severely wound- ed during the . Commune, and being disabled for further ser- vice in the'army, was retired on a pension. He

lIE LATE MAX O'RELL.

then came- tc , London, where 1-1- first acted as correspondent for French newspapers, and later became French master at St. Paul's School, a position which he held from 1876 to 1884. It was while a master at St. Paul's that he published "John Bull d His Isiand," the first of mclre than a 'dozen books, all light, humorous, and good- natured, which,- translated into Euglish by his wile, obtained for him a wide-spread Popularity as an author. Among them may be mentioned "The Dear Neighbours:" " Drat the 80y5,,, "John Bull and Company," •' Between Ourselves," and "Rambles in Womanland," the last having been published 'quite recently. M. Bluet was a successful lecturer in the United Kingdom, France, and America, and his experiences on tour afforded 3naterial for an abundance of bright copy." He spent several seasons in the United States, and recorded his impressions in "Jonathan and his Continent," published in 1889. M. tlouet travelled in Australia, New Zealand, and South Africa, and some ,time before his ieath acted as a European correspondent of a New-York paper.

DEMONS'

;TRATION CROWD:

of life has never ceasea to flow. At the Duke of York's School is a miniature regi- ment, inheriting soldierly instincts, and con- scious of the lofty traditions it has to maintain. They parade like old soldiers; they are clad in historic scarlet; they have passed in review every year before the greatest chieftains of the army; they carry colours similar to those borne by infantry regiments, a right conferred upon them by King George IV. in 1825. The Duke of York, the founder, continued to be a constant visitor to the shhool to the day of his death, and our Royal Family have ever since shown their interest in the school.. It was in the year 1897 that the then Duke and Duchess of Corn- wall and York presented new coloars to the boys on behalf of Queen Victoria. The last days of the school have been clouded by an outbreak of scarlet fever, and in consequence it has been decided t e temporarily close the buildings and remove the scholars tc Grange Fort, Portsmouth.

MR. HANBURY'S SUCCESSOR.

The Earl of Onslow, who succeeds the late Mr. Hanbury at the Board of Agriculture, is a very popular and hard-working politician, who has made his way to the top and into the Cabinet by years of hard work. Fifty- three years of age, he succeeded to the title in 1870 at his great-uncle's death. Re first held office nnder Lord Salisbury as a Lord-in- Waiting in 1880, and again in his 1886 Adminis- tration. In 1887 he was made Under- Secretary for the Colonies, but was transferr%d a year later to the Board of Trade as Par- liamentary Sec- retary. Then he spent four years in New Zealand as its

Governor,. and upon -coming home was appointed Under - Secretary for India. In 'goo he was transferred once more to the Colonial Office, and all through the war and the troubles involved he was Mr. Cham- berlain's right-hand man. Lord Onslow is a keen practical agriculturist, and, therefore, well suited to his new office.

FOR JACK ASHORt.

The secretary/ of the new Sailors' Palace en the Commercial Road, Whitechapel, the Rev. Edward Matthews, the veteran secretary of the old institution, hit upon a most felicitous description in his speech. "The palace is," he said, "a first-class ocean liner, launched on a mission of mercy." The palace is the headquarters of the British and Foreign Sailors' Society, and comes .as a direct successor of the old sloop-of-war "Speedy," which was opened in the .Thames in 18i8 and supplanted in i 856 by the Sailors' Institute at Shadwell. It is the gift of that well - known philanthropist. Mr. Passmore Edwards, and is beautifully equipped throughout. One of the admirable

He made a very neat and impressive little speech, and thanked Mr. Passmore Edwards in the name of all present. It was quite a sailors' day, and even Whitechapel looked quite bright and gay.

THE PARIS FLAT MURDER.

An unusual amount of interest has beet aroused by the murder of Mdle. Bertha de Brienne in her flat in Paris, partly by the fact that she was a well-known character, and partly because the man who stands charged with the crime was arrested in Glasgow. This has made the sensation a Bri- tish one, and has excited pub- lic opinion oyez here.. The mur- dered woman was celebrated for her jewellery which she used to wear every night at the supper rooms she frequented, and this in the end proved hex undoing, as, whoever mur- dered her, the motive for the crimewas clearly SEM the acquisition of her valuable This

property.

GIVEN UP.

How important is the action of the kidneys on the health of the body is continually proved by the many diseases which are cured by the rational treatment of the-se organs. Mr. Gayland Lambert, of 7, Adamson-street, Padiham Lancashire, was struck dawn with Rheumatic Fever and after six months of medical treatment, was given up. All his friends despaired of ever seeing him well again. Prior to this yery serious illness;Mr. Lambert had been troubled with Insomnia, so thathis condition at the outset was very low and weak. Help came to Mr. Lambert when he commenced to take Dodd's Kidney Pills. The real trouble was the kidneys, and Dodd's Kidney Pills speedily began to set them right. Once the kidneys were made well, Mr. Lambert steadily began to improve. He was able to sleep soundly at regular intervals, his strength began to return and the fever quickly left him. This is the experience of many who take Dodd's Kidney Pills. In curing the kidneys they make them do their duty and cleanse the body of impurities in the blood. The consequence is that many people who have suffered for years from weak kidneys are cured of old standing complaints which they never realised as due solely to the inactive condition of the kidneys.

HANDICAP AT EARLESTOWN.

• The annual handicap at bowls for the Viaduct Insti- tute Committee's prize,and the entrance fees, took place on the Institute greens on Saturday afternoon. The weather was of a delightful character, and the greens were in excellent condition, the result being that the contests were of a keen description. There were 128 entries. The first prize was taken by J. Booth, 2nd, J. Bedworth; 3, J. Bailey 4, W. Eaton, There were four other prizes of ss. each, all the prizes consisting of orders on tradesmen.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Baptist Church, Colborne-street.

In the absence of the pastor, Mr. John Davies of War- rington, preached morning and evening at the above church, taking for his text at the evening service the words, "But if not, be it known unto thee, 0 king, that we will not serve thy gods, nor worship the golden image which thou hast set up." Men who dared to do the right in the face of danger, the preacher stated always had a fascination for us, and although it had been said that every man could be bought, that every man had his price, there were some men who would prefer to lose all rather than be false to themselves and I to their God. in urging his congregation to be trueat all costs he pointed out that nobleness of character I could only be gained by the way of the cross. The holy life, however, was not an impossible one, and in ; spite of the many difficulties we could be "more than conquerors through Christ who loved us and gave Him- self for us."

===== 0002601_19030529_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
Bishop of Liverpool at Warrington.

On Sunday afternoon the Bishop of Liverpool preached at St. Ann's Church and in the evening at St. Peter's both services being well attended. The service at St. Peter's, which was in aid of Church repairs, was con- ducted by the Vicar (Rev. B. Percival) and the Curate (Rev. D. R. James).Referring to the objects particularly needing repair, the Bishop said that first it was needful front time to time to paint the outside of the building, and, secondly, the kneelers wanted re-covering. He knew they were not over blessed with this world's goods, but in the past they had given well and unselfishly to maintain that house of God, and he was quite sure that phat they had done in the past they would do in tini future. The collection amounted to t 4 Os. 11d. After the service the members of the choir were complimented on their excellent singing by his Lordship.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe Cockhedge Mission Anniversary. .

- Successful Sunday school anniversary services were held at the Wycliffe Cockhedge Congregational Mission, Brick-street, on Sunday. The room had been prettily decorated by Mrs. J. Atkinson (who provided the fidwers) and the Misses Clayton. The anthems were "Ye shall dwell in the land" (J. Stainer) and "Father of Mercies" (John E. West). Mr. Frank Berry was entrusted with the solos in the former, and Miss Walsh (of Bolton) with those in the latter, and their singing was all that could be desired. The Mission choir was supplemented by several members of Wycliffe choir,and their combined efforts, under the able conductorship of Mr. John Atkinson, were much appreciated. Miss Hall rendered efficient service at the organ. In the morning the preacher was Councillor John Upson, who in his discourse said a world without Christ was a world with ignorance. It was necessary that a child should have every capacity for learning, but though he might be educated in the highest degree, yet, it was possible to be ignorant in regard to God. Edu- cation had been tried as the remedy for sin and crime, but it had failed. A world without Christ was also a cruel world, as might be seen in the dark places of heathen lands. Furthermore, a world without Christ degenerated both morally and spiritually. Where Christ was unknown there was none of those joys of family love, none of those sweet consolations in death, no joys or brightness; nothing but sullen gloom. A service of song, entitled "Florence Nightingale "was creditably rendered in the afternoon. The connective readings were read by Miss Whitfield. Miss Walsh, Miss Lucy Pearson, and Miss Polly Hackett contributed solos, and Miss Alice Griffiths and Mr. Harry Griffiths gave a duet.—Councillor James Evans occupied the pulpit in the evening. Selfishness he said was the greatest curse. Every form of evil proceeded from a selfish source. There was a great need to overcome selfishness, and it behoved them to strive to overcome it. If they did so, there was the reward—a mansion in God's Eternal House—no sorrow, no pain, no death—a place of joy and peace, and love.—The offertories amounted to over R 8:

Baptist Church, Earlestown. The annual special services in connection with the anniversary of the Earlestown. Baptist Sunday school were held on Sunday in the church. The weather being in every way propitious, there were large congregations at each of the servioes. The Rev. H. C. Williams, of Corwen, occupied the pulpit and preached homely but instructive sermons at the morning and evening services. Special hymns, anthems, and solos were sung, the principal soloists being Misses G. Hume, S. Morris, E. Wells, F. Potts, P. Morris, B. Eckersley, E. Jones, B. Grissold, and Master C. Eckersley.—ln the afternoon the choir,assisted by a specially trainednhoir of children submitted the cantata 'David, the Shepherd Boy." Miss Harvey presided at the organ, and Mr. S. Harvey conducted. The singing was of a high order, and gave the greatest satisfaction. The collections throughout , the day amounted to about £53 in aid of the Sunday ischool funds.

CHESHIRE COUNTY COUNCIL.

A quarterly meeting of this council was held at Chester Castle on Thursday last week. There was a full attend- ice of members, and on the proposition of Mr. Jthn Thompson, seconded by Dr. D. B. Hewitt (Northwich), Col. Dixon was unanimously elected chairman for the eleventh time. Mr. Beeley was re-appointed to the vice-chair.—The Chairman made a feeling allusion to the death of Mr. Hanbury (minister for agriculture), remarking that agriculture in the County of C.lesterhad sustained a severe loss.

THE RUNCORN AMALGAMATION SCHEME.

It w-a-e—rei;;;ted--that the Local Government Act Committee, having considered a memorial from the Runcorn Urban District Council, applying for an order extending the boundaries of the urban district by in- cluding therein the rural parishes of Halton and Weston, had resolved "That, as in the opinion of this committee a prima facie case has been made out for altering the boundaries of the urban district of Runcorn by includ- ing therein the parishes of Halton and Weston, a com- mitte be and is hereby appointed, consisting of Messrs. C. H. Booth, John Thompson and William Hodgson, to hold a local inquiry into the matter and to report thereon." _

The council confirmed the recommendation.

Unique Co-operative Society. The Chief Registrar of Industrial and Provident Societies has notified the dissolution of the Lowton Common Co-operative Industrial Society, Ltd., which for many years carried on business at the village of Lowton, near Newton-le-Willows. In die early days of the co-operative movement about twenty persons combined to purchase and divide groceries, and as the society's business grew thepembers took it in turn to act as shopmen, thus saving the expense of paid employees, this system obtaining throughout the society's existence.

BIRKENHEAD SHow.--Intending exhibitors are reminded that entries in the dog, poultry, and pigeon sections at the forthcoming show of the Wirral and Birkenhead Agricultural Society, to be held at Bebing- ton next month, positively close by Saturday's post (30th inst.). Any further information will be supplied on applying to the secretary, at 6, Hamilton-square, Birkenhead.

BOWLING.

LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE COUNTY ASSOCIATION. SPLENDID VICTORY OF THE WARRINGTON TEAU.

WARRINGTON (CEMETERY) V. STRETFORD (ANGEL).— I The Warrington (Cemetery) team is doing splendidly in the County Association Competition. Their victory over Newton-le-Willows a fortnight ago was followed on Saturday by a. meritorious win over Stretford. The match was played at Kersal Moor. Warrington had not, their str*mgest available team, for several players who are well known at these annual competitions were absenbees. The start from a Warrington point of view was not a promising one, for in the first quartette of players T. Cooper, usually a reliable player, never looked like winning his game with 0. Kulow, and suffered defeat. That consistently good bowler, T. Bridge, reduced the leeway somewhat in his game with G. Drinkwater. W. Hill emulated Bridge's example by disposing of Alsop. A close game was next wit- nessed between G. Ball and R. D. Lowe (captain of the , Stretford team), the Warringtonian winning by two points. Thus at the finish of the first quartette of Idames the Warringtonians led by five shots. T. Jaoksonrecovered the lost ground for Stretford, when he defeated W. Pave)', who losing the jack at th 7th end failed to add to his record, and the game was called 21-12 in Jackson's favour. A dlngdong game was Oust betweenE. Taylor and J. Poynton. For the major portion of the game Taylor indalged in a useful lead until the 21st end, when the . game was called 14 all. Poynton then took the lead, but the advantage gained was not maintained and securing the jack at the 23rd end. Taylor ran out a winner by six shots. The War- rington captain, T. Bolton, set his men a rare example when he defeated A. Twiss by eight points. Bolton played consistently throughout, bowling a good length and practically never allowed the issue to be in doubt. One of the most exciting games of the afternoon was that played between H. Williams and J. Valentine sen.' and at the close the odd shot separated the players, the game being called 21-20 in Williams's favour. At the close of these series of games Warrington enjoyed a lead of eleven on the aggregate played. Eight of these were lost when J. Woods succumbed to J. Whitehead and the issue of the match at this period had a very open appearance. T. Ellison however, came to the rescue of his side, for taking the lead from the commencement, his opponent, D. Wigglesworth, was always in difficulties. The game in fact was a runaway, affair, and Ellison was credited with the most substan- tial win of the afternoon winning by the large margin of 14 points. Some exciting sport was witnessed in the game between J. Ashton and M. Chalmers, and though each trundler took the lead in turn the issue looked in safe keeping when the game was called 20.18 in favour of Ashton. Chalmers, however, secured the jack and won •by the odd chalk. A similar state of affairs was the result of the game between J. Harrison and J. Valen- tine, and victory went in favour of the Stretford man by two points. The Warringtonians thus wan seven games out of the twelve played, and gained their entry into the third round. Scare : •

THE EXAMINER.

MAY 29th, 1903.

NOTHING was said THE MOTOR CAR in either House of Par-

MADNESS. liament on Tuesday oncerning the proposed motor race in Ireland yet amongst members both of the House of Lords and the House of Commons there must, as among the general public, be a considerable amount of anxiety and apprehension lest we should have in "alster country, on a somewhat smaller scale indeed %repetition of the tragedies which have given so Irightful a celebrity to the motor car race in prance. The enthusiasts in the cause are giving themselves great pains to prove that there can be 1)0 resemblance between the Irish race and that t'vhieh has had such shocking results on French 4:11. It is pointed out that the cars will far less numerous, that the interval :tween each will be wider, that the (113:ktsters will be kept out of danger by sol- ,:ers and police, and that, therefore, all risks "11 be confined to persons actually competing In the race. These arguments are not quite con- Vlncing. If a motor-car swerved into a hedge

behind which there was a row of spectators, or if it became eccentric in its behavour on the Curragh,

where the public are to be kept at a regulated dis-

ance, its speed would probably be such as would Produce a catastrophe in which not merely the occupants of the car would be involved. What will probably prove to be the strongest argument 'ln favour of the race is the fact that Ireland cannot afford to lose the money that she has already Ilvested in connection with the event. The Other contention that the ground to be raced over Irelani is vastly shorter than the distance 'from Paris to Madrid is, of course, of no conse- ' Ilene() whatever, seeing that the French race was brought to a premature con- cluSlOfl that the accidents all oceured at °4rious stages, on the first section of the chosen route. Apart from consideration of the loss to Ireland, it will be generally felt, we believe, that the race for the GORDON-BENNETT Cup ought not to take place. "There will be no more motor- say the principal members of a great firm of motor-car builders. The truth seems to be that

the most daring and enthusiastic of the com- Potitors in the French race have been shocked and

'terrified, which is but natural. If the race had been continued to Madrid it is quite probable that the few competitors who reached the Spanish

*eaPital mould have been in a state of nervous .°llapse. For over a portion of the covered oute the pace reached the almost in- credible rate of nearly 90 miles per hour. Yhe accidents would have almost certainly Increased in number in proportion :to the extent of ground covered—and what is all this for? What i 8 the problem to be solved by the curious 'acing of motor-cars over country roads? It the problem of hew a motor-maniac may commit

Bwift suicide. This is absolutely no comm ercial or 'atilitarian purpose tr 4 be served.No body desires to transport either himself or his goods over unpre- Pared roads at a rate of 88 miles per hour, or if 'There are such persons they should be put under .severe restraint. The sad events in Franco will inevitably exercise a great influence over the Bill vhich Al[r. 'WALTER LONG IS to introduce to Parlia- ent• So far as we know at present that measure 48 to leave the question of speed to the discretion of the drivers of motor-cars. Obviously,they can- "ll(3t be trusted to exercise their discretion. They 12,118 h on death or they cause the death of others as Iv' they were soldiers engaged in a battle. The t.renob motor race has shown us the danger in Irne• What the motorist requires is restraint, t the liberty to go at whatever pace he may Please.

IT was a day of small THE VETO OF THE things in the House LORDS. of Commons on Wednesday . The I°l-Ise no longer adjourns for the Derby, but most (a)( Its members go down to Epsom nevertheless, .incl the green benches are practically deserted. the 5' in fact, impossible on Wednesday to carry hnsiness on till the trrival of the dinner hour. 0 t the evening sitting Mr. CREMER found an Pertunity to move in a thin House 4, That the v'nlver now possessed by the House of Lords to et° an measure adopted by the House of

Commons should be limited to one session of Parliament." This would be a really sweeping reform. The Lords would have their teeth drawn with a vengeance if a law embodying IMr. CREMKR'S motion were V) find its way Fon to the statute book. They would then be able to reject a measure once, but not to repeat therejection in the next or any succeeding year. They could exercise a suspensory power; only they would have no power of ultimate rejection. The House of Lords of the future would become an entirely different thing to the House of Lords of the past. If the power of the Upper Chamber had in 1892 been such as Mr. CREMER now desires to make them, Ireland would at this moment have been living under Home Rule, for Mr. GLADSTONE would again have brought for- ward his defeated measure in 1893 and the House lof Lords would then have had no power to reject it. Under Mr.CREMER'S proposal the Education Act would lose a great deal of its objectionableness. Its evil features could be removed within a year or so of the first change of Government. The House of Lords would stand by the priests, of course, but it could only stand by them during one session of Parliament. In the following session the House of Commons and the will of the people would prevail. Mr. C REMER would, in fac practically achieve the abolition of the House of Lords as an impediment to legislation. Nobody need then desire either to remove it or improve it, to end it or mend it. It would become in reality what some of its supporters and eulogists main- tain that it now is, merely a barrier to hasty legis- lation. There would be no !more ploughing of the sands in the Lower House,for ploughing the sand, means passing measures which the House of Lords is certain to reject again and again until they are sent up by a Government of which it approves. The late EA.RL of SELBORNE once de- scribed the Upper Chamber as a committee room of the Carlton Club. The description is as accur- ate as it is apt. The House of Lords registers the decisions of the Tory party, whatever those may happen to be. If a Liberal Government is in office it reverses the result of the election :which placed it there,and denies to the people precisely thatwhich they determined upon when their votes returned a Liberal majority. That the House of Lords ever exercises a suspensory power on the ground that measures have not been sufficiently considered is a senseless superstition opposed to all actual ex- perience. If Parliament ever gets into a panic the House of Lords gets into a panic first. Whenever it sits late to re- ceive Bills from the House of Commons it adopts hastily what has been hastily framed else- where. Itis that which has been well-considered both by the people and in Parliament that the House of Lords obstructs. Mr. GLADSTONE saw uery clearly in his latter days that the Upper House hasj become a permanent bar to Liberal legislation. This was the meaning of the last speech which he made in the House of Commons, and it will always be debatable whether the Liberal party did not make a mistake of vast magnitude when it declined to follow the path which he pointed out on the final day of his leader- ship. Mr. CREMER on Wednesday night argued for a more moderate kind of proceeding than would have satisfied Mr. GLADSTONE, but his plan would be a very good makeshift if it were realis- able, and it would accelerate legislation almost beyond belief.

Passive resistance has begun. The Rev. J. F Matthews of Warrington, has replied to the Corporation demand note for the rates by a refusal to pay that portion which is levied for educational purposes. In an eKplanatory letter to the Borough Collector, Mr. Matthews treats the obnoxious Act with scant ceremony, and avers that he never intends "to contribute towards the obsequies of Nonconform- ity even in Warrington." In conclusion he says: "I fear therefore, you will have to take the only course that remains for securing that portion of the rate I refuse to pay." The" only course" is for Mr. Matthews to be sum- moned before the magistrates who would have to issue a distress warrant which the Corporation would execute. The other day,when we saw the rev gentleman be did notseem at all concerned about his impending fate or the ultimate destination of his goods and chattels.

Warrington Corporation has been so severely criticised of late that it is a pleadure to be able to bestow a meed of praise when it has been justly deserved, The Gas Committee have an excellent account to give of their stewardship during the past twelve months. To be able to show a profit of over £B,OOO after meeting all charges, including interest and sinking fund, is a record of which they may well be proud, and one which is likely to make other Corporation committees envious. After such a satisfactory year, the long suffering gas consumer may surely look for some concession. The price of gas in Warrington cannot be said to be exorbit- ant at 2s. 7d. per thousand cubic feet, but it is still much higher than our near neighbours at Widnes pay,where the cheapest gas in the country is manufactured by the Corporation. A slight reduction in Warrington would be greatly appreciated and it would come with additional grace after the handsome coptribution which the Gas Committee has made in relief of rates. We should also like to see an improvement in the public lighting of the town. Since Cou_neillor James Evans became convener of the lighting sub-committee an appreciable im- provement has been made in the lighting of the main thoroughfares by the introduction of the incandescent burners. This improved system of lighting might now be extended to all the street lamps in the Borough and as the Gas Committee has a substantial balance in hand, the present is the most opportune moment for broaching the subject.

The punctiliousness of the English law is often amusing. We have recently rezeived the follow- ing from the liquidator to a company in bank- ruptcy :—" I beg to inform you that I am now in a position, with the sanction of the Court, to pay a third and final dividend to the creditors of this company of three-halfpence in the pound. A cheque (or stamps) for 2d, being the dividend at the rate aforesaid, is enclosed herewith. Please return this notice with the receipt on the back signed." As the dividend is so unexpected and precious we have not extravagantly spent half of it in acknowledging receipt.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

FATHER AND SON IN TROUBLE.

At the Runcorn Police Court on Saturday before Messrs. F. J. Norman and E. Imison, Joseph Basnett, Fryer-street, was committed to gaol for a fortnight on two counts that of being drunk and disorderly and of assaulting Ann Gilligan, Fryer-street on Thursday night last.—Joseph Basnett, prisoner's father, was fined ss. and costs, for being drunk on Friday morning. He went to the Police Station to inquire about his sonand refused to go away.—Basnett junior, it was stated, had been an inmate of Upton Asylum and an allowance of 7s. 6d. weekly had been made to him from the poor rate.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
ASCENSION DAX AT THE PARISH CHURCH.

On Ascension Day the combined choirs of the Parish and St. Michael's churches gave the second portion of "The Redemption" (Gouuod). The choruses were finely rendered, but the most enjoyable portion of the evening may be set clown to the solos taken by Master Sam Wooley. This youth is the possessor of a remarkable voice both in compass and tone and he delivered in a thoroughly artistic manner the some- what difficult composition "They who seek things eternal." There was a good congregation and Mr. lioraston, the trainer, is to be complimented on the executive power displayed by the singers.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
RETURN OF MR. KIRKHAM EVANS.

Runcornians will be interested to learn that the above gentleman; who, some twenty years ago, occu- pied a rather prominent position in the town intends to return for a six months' holiday to Frodsham where his sister at present resides. When at Runcorn he ran a drapery establishment but at the present time he is permanent secretary to "Our Boys' institute,"Adelaide, Australia. In that country Mr. Evans has had a most successful career and his return is anticipated with much pleasure by his old friends. Prior to his depar- ture he was presented with a purse of gold by the presi- dent (Mr. P. Wood) of the Institute.

RUNCORN HOMING SOCIETY.

The members of the Runcorn Horning Society flew the second old bird race on Saturday from Worcester. The distance was 85 miles and the wind west. Three hundred and eighty-four birds competed. The follow- ing was the result :—Dyer, 1,026; Shaw and Sons, 1,026; Dyer, 1,005; Farrel, 993; W. Shaw, 983; Edwards, 981; Simpson Bros., 978; Dyer, 978; Lea, 973; Johnson and Sons, 965; Howard, 961; Shaw and Sons, 958; Lea, 955; Killcross, 948; Marshall, 946; Shaw and Sons (3) 944; W. WHITSUNTIDE POSTAL kRRANGEMENTS.

On Whit-Monday, June Ist, there will he one delivery only of letters mid parcels, that in the morning, both town and rural districts. .There will be one despatch only of letters and parcels, that at nine p.m. Letters will be collected from the letter boxes in the rural dis- tricts immediately on completion of the postmen's deliveries in the morning, and from the town letter boxes in the evening at the times shown on each box. On Whit-Tuesday, June 2nd, the afternoon cellection from town sub-offices and the 4.20 p.m. delivery in town will be suspended. The evening delivery to Halton, Weston, and Weston Point will take place as usual. On both these days the town sub-offices will be closed.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
SAD DROWNING FATMATY.

On Tuesday afternoon Mr. R. Dobson, coroner, held an inquest at Runcorn on the body of William Abram, of the coasting flat Beale, who was found drowned in tke locks of the Bridgewater Canal on Monday. Ellen Fisher, of 4, Queen-street, said the deceased was her brother, and aged 49 years. He lived with her when on shore, and was at home on Saturday night. He had had some drink, but she had seen him worse. He took offence because she brought him a candle to go to bed. She pulled his shoes off twice, but he again put them on and went out at 11 40 p.m., stating he was going to his ship. She never saw him alive again. Evidence was also given by Mr. 'P. Bylield and Police-constable Mottershead,and the jury returned a verdict of "Found drowned."

===== 0002601_19030529_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD.

The monthly meeting of the above Board was held on Tuesday evening, Mr. Pritchard in the chair.—After formal business a communication was read from the County Council re the appointment of managers on the local education authority. After A-private discussion it was resolved on the motion of Mr. Handley, seconded by Canon WOod that in the opinion of the Board it was advisable that the Runcorn Council (meaning Board) schools should be grouped under one board of managers, and in the event of this being accepted by the County Education authority they further suggest that the selection should be made from the membersOf the Runcorn School Board.—Copies of the resolution were forwarded to the County authority.On the recom- mendation of the School Management Committee it was decided to give testimonials to the officers and teachers.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
A PITIA BLE. CASE.

At the Runcorn Petty Sessions, on Monday, before Messrs. F. J. Norman (in the chair) and E. Imison, David Newport, St. john's-street, was summoned for causing his two boys unnecessary suffering.—P.C. Edwards deposed that on the 20th of the present month at 7 a.m.. he went to St. John's-street in consequence of information given at the Police Station. In the yard ho saw the two lads sleeping on a board with only abag to cover them. They had no clothes on, and they said they had been turned out at 5.30 that morning when the father went to work. They had been turned out every morning for a week. The boys produced two dry crusts and stated that they had only eaten similar crusts for the week previous—about one a day. The defendant said that he had had some trouble with his wife and would look after his children in future.—The Bench adjourned the case for a month to see if the de- fendant would look after his children properly.

The monthly meeting of the above committee was heldon Friday under the presidency of Mr. Handley. After general business the librarian (Mr. Jones) sub- mitted a balance sheet for the year 1902-1903. The expenditure included:— Salary £6O, magazines and newspapers £3O 55., books £33 14s. 4d., bookbinding £33 13s. 10d.. printing and stationery £6 16s. 5d., fittings and furniture £5 95.; sundries £8 ss. Bd., rent £36. The balance remaining in hand was £8 is. 9d. The financial statement was approved. it was also resolved that the library be closed on Whit-Monday and Tuesday and that for stock- taking the library be closed from June 25th to July 13th
