The Examiner Friday, May 29, 1903.

RUNCORN FOOTBALL CLUB.

Annual Meeting.

The annual meeting of the above club was held in Vicar-street schoolroom on Wednesday evening undet the presidency of Mr. S. Taylor. There was a very good gathering of members.

THE SECRETARY'S REPORT.

Mr. Ellison, after touching on the satisfactory manner in which Mr. Platt, secretary to the League,had arranged the fixtures stated that during the season seventeen new players had been signed on. Injuries to Houghton, Jolley, Price, Butterworth, Richardson, and Williams disarranged matters towards the end of the season making the forward line not so powerful as formerly. The County match, between Cheshire and Lan- cashire was a distinct success and the County Com_ mittee will probably honour Irwell-lane similarly next winter. Runcorn supplied ten and eleven players in the matches. Runcorn did not survive the fateful fourth round of the Northern Union Cup-tie, succumbing to Halifax by a luckily placed goal. For twenty years the Club had been trying to win the South-West Lancashire Cup and had failed. But the past season had seen Runcorn holding a good position throughoutand the trophy was won by seventy points to ten. In the Northern League out of 34 matches played, 19 were won, 11 lost, 4 drawn, the club finishing fourth on the list. Owing to a demonStmtion against the referee on November Bth, the ground was suspended for three weAs, a sentence which had a very adverse bearing on Runcorn's chance for the League championship. The works competition had been a distinct success. The team had only been de- feated twice at home, the attack being as good as ever but the defence having slightly deteriorated. Total number of matches played, 38; won, 21; drawn, 5; points scored, 278; opponents score,l47. The follow- ing scored tries :—Moran, 16; Butterworth,l4; Richard- son, 10; Walker, 7; D. Jones, 6; Price, 5 ' • Jolley, 3; Langley, Woods, Warder,Flannagan, T. Woocls,one each. H. Farmer placed 26 goals from tries, 3 from penalties, and 2 from marks, 31 goals in all. S. Houghton, one penalty goal and one 'from a mark. Jolley dropped 6 goals and Price one goal. .

- The work of the s-econd team was reviewed by Mr. Abel, assistant secretary who stated that 26 matches had been played, 13 won, 7 lost, 6 drawn. Points scored 175 against 87. The play had been very good on the whole. In the Lancashire Combination 18 matches had been won 8 lost, 4 drawn, scoring 130 points against 6.9. In the friendly matches 175 were scored against 87.

The raports were Approved on the motion of Messrs. W. Jones and Robinson. THE TREASURER'S REPORT.

The treasurer, Mr. Pownall, in his report, drew atten- tion to the decrease of members subscriptions by 27, principally owing to the Northern Union rule, that, ticket-holders must pay at least 10s. Gd. per year to wit- ness cup tie matches. The stand tickets showed a decrease by 8, but season tickets an increase of 19. In all under the three heads there was a deficiency of £5 Bs. Gd. The gate and stand receipts for League and Friendly matches had gone up by £152 ss. 2d.; Northern Union Cup ties produced £264 us. 2d, being £47 9s. 9d. in excess of the previous year. The works competition, an item which did nut appear last year, brought in £10625. Bd. The overdraft at Parr's Bank was only £29 14s. 10d. compared with £62 4s. 7d. last year Players insurance showed an increase of £lO Ns. Travelling expenses had also increased by £l6 17s. id. and lost time by £6 17s. 10d. The sum of £62 Gs. id. had been spent for pants itc. against £73 188. 3d. last season. Postages had decreased by £1 3s. 3d. Doctors fees had gone up by £9 Ss. id. Printing had increased to £46 9s, Bd. being £lO 9s. 9d. more than the previous season. The club had made £25 12s. 2d. against £BO 7s. 7d. in the 1901-02 season. The works competition cleared £67 lls, the expenses amounting to £3B Us. Bd. There was to credit £l4l 18s. 5d., against £129 12s. 3d. the financial position being improved by the sum of £l2 6s. 2d., the liabilities for the year being less by £33 12s. 3d.

Mr. Shore proposed and Mr. T. Cooke seconded the adoption of the report. Mr. Donnellan pointed out that there had been a fall ing off in one of the items of .C 5. This was due to the raising of the subscription from 10s. (id, to 12s. (Id.. Could not the meeting alter this rule? The Chairman pointed out that a 21 days notice was required before any rule could be altered. Mr. Donnellan stated that the balance sheet had only been put in his hands a week ago so that he had not had any chance to move in the matter.

The report was approved. ELECTION OF OFFICERS.

On the motion.- Oi-ifr: o-n-es-tii;clMr. Dudley, Mr. S. Taylor was unanimously re-elected president. The Chairman in thanking them for the honour. eulogised the work of the team and the committeeand hoped for better times next season. Mr. Pownall was unanimously re-elected treasurer OR the motion of Messrs. Linaker and Moulton.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTION OF SECRETARY.

The Chairman!said he was sorry that there were two nominations for this position. He had had a deal to do with Mr. Ellison and hB was sure a better secretary could not be obtained. He had always done his • work. well. He had.been secretary and assistant secretary for 16 years and 6 years secretary to the club. • On the vote being taken Mr. Ellison was re-elected by 103 votes to Mr. Abel 69. Mr. Ellison in thanking the members said the work at the latterpart of the season had by no means been a bed of roses, and unless he perfectly enjoyed the con. fldence of the committee in the future he would have to seriously considerhis position. Mr. Alfred Abel was unanimously appointed assistant secretary on the motion of Messrs. Lightburn and Jones.

The following appointments were also made : Captain first team, J. Butterworth; vice-captain, J. Richardson captain A. team, H. Jones ; sub-captain,? Faulkner.

Committee: Messrs. Jones Dudley, G. Robinson, H. Cawley, G. Wainwright, A. Abel, T. Price, H. Grounds, R. Garnett, R. Briscoe, Moulton, G. Moores. The meeting requested the committee on the motion of Mr. Donnellan to fix the annual general meeting at such a date after the distribution of the annual balance sheet as would enable members to comply with rule 18 which states that no alteration shall be made to any of the rules except at a general meeting of the club of which a written notice of any proposed alteration shall be given in waiting to the secretary three weeks before hand.

Votes of thanks to the Chairman &c., closed the meeting.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

As there is every indication of a fine week-end, the prospects for the success of the Earlestown May Queen festival are of a rosy character. The public are enter- ing into the affair with great zest, and it is anticipated that the show on Saturday will eclipse anything which has hitherto taken place in the town. There is sure to be a big influx of visitors and it will be necessary for the committee to strain every effort to present a festival which will be a credit to the town, and be worthy of the trouble of the visitors in attending it.

There is good news for gas consumers. At the present time we are paying the Council 3s. 21. per 1000 feet for our gas, but it is understood that the profit on the undertaking is such that the committee have decided to reduce it by 4d. per 1000 feet, and this with an allow- ance of 6d. per 1000 if the account is paid within a month, puts the charge on a reasonable basis,and reduces itto nearly the level of what it was prior to the in- crease. No doubt the committee will receive the silent thanks of the consumers.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
CRICKET.

After an interval of about five years, the quarrel between the Earlestown and Newton Cricket Clubs, has this year been patched up, and these great rivals met again on Saturday on the Earlestown ground to try conclusions in a friendly encounter. The keenest_ inter- est was taken in the fixture, and both teams were certain of victory, but the prevailing opinion amongst the public was that Earlestown would win. In all the previous meetings of the clubs, Earlestown have asserted their superiority, but forthe first time Newton loweied their colours in a manner beyond dispute. Of course. some excuse may be offered for the defeat of Earles- town in the fact that Miller was an absentee having injured himself the Wednesday previous, playing for Newton against Wigan.

The weather was beautifully fine, and consequently a large crowd of spectators turned up. It is pleasing to note, however, that I everything passed off most agreeably and there was no trace of ill-feeling amongst either the spectators or players. Earlestown batted first arid their !total score stood at 168. It was freely stated that the I Newton players would be unable to come anywhere near this, in fact it was thought if they got the odd 68 I they would do well. They, however, were not dis- heartened by the big score, and went to the wickets Iwith the air of men determined to win. As the game progressed the excitement increased and when the win- lning hit was made, the elation of the Newton supporters was beyond question. The fielding of the Earlestown team was bad and it was due to this that they suffered defeat by two wickets and 23 runs. The return fixture will be Played on the Newton ground on July 4th.

A Doctor's Claim. At the Warrington County Court on Thursday before his Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C.,•Dr. Walter Latham, of ,Earlestown, claimed get ss. 6d. from John Lythgoe, ganger, of 29, Derby-street, Earlestown, formerly of Mount Tabor, Newton, for medical attendance.--Mr, E. A, White, solicitor, re- presented the plaintiff and pointed out that Dr. Latham attended two persons who lived with the de- fendant, one of whom passed off as his wife—The de- fendant said the person alluded to was his housekeeper at the time, but he had since married her. He never asked the plaintiff to attend tp her.--His Honour held ,that the defendant was responsible for the bill and gave judgment for the plaintiff.

EARLESTOWN -AND NEWTON.

NEWTON OCCASIONAL COURT

At Newton Police Station' on Monday morning, before Mr. T. J. Gillespie, James Hilton and Pat Tracey.were charged with being drunk and disorderly in Newton.—Hilton was fined 3s. 6d.and Tracey ss. and costs.—Pat Carey was fined 10s. and costs for a similar offence, and John Riley 2s. 6d. and costs.—Thomas Quince was also charged with being drunk in Earlestown and he was fined 2s. 6d. and costs.

HAYDOCK COLLIERS ENDANGER .THEIR LIVES. flj

On Thursday at the Newton-le-Willows Police CouiT, Walter Rowlands, collier, of Haydock, pleaded guilty to having entered a working placecontaining gas in the Princess pit, Haydock, after it had been marked by the danger board by a fireman. He was fined ss. and costs. —Thomas Peppard, Richard Pilling, Joseph Smethurst, and Henry Cotton, colliers employed at Messrs. R. Evans and Co.'s Wood pit, Haydock, were fined ss. and costs each for sitting on the top of tubs whilst riding • to their working places. A WARNING TO COLLIERY STOKERS.

__ • Alfred Bacon, stoker, of Collins Green, ear St, Helens, was on Thursday at Newton Police Court fined 40s. and costs for a breach of the special rules by going to the Collins Green Colliery on the 15th May in a state of intoxication. Mr. E. Peace, who prosecuted, stated that a lot of trouble with stokers had been ex- perienced, and as this was a flagrant case, defendant having abused the en.ineer,heaprpealed for the infliction of the maximum penalty. • WHIT-WEEK HOLIDAY.

In consequence of the various works holding their annual holiday in August, very little'notice is taken of Whitsuntide in Earlestown and Newton, although the tradespeople make a general holiday of Whit Monday. This year, however, the Viaduct Works are departing from the general rule, and are giving the workmen a holiday by closing on Thursday evening until Monday morning. If the present fine weather should continue, there is sure to be a large exodus of people who, with the privilege of travelling at quarter fare, can so cheaply reach the various popular resorts.

ST. HELENS

ST. HELENS RATES.--THREEPENCE INCREASE ONLY REQUIRED.

The overseers met on Thursday, and found that they were,able to pract4cally reduce last year's is. 3d. poor rate by 14d. It has been decided to lay a borough-rate of 3s. 4d. against 3s. last year, and apoor-rate and high- way-rate of is. 11d. against Is. last year, so that the total rates of the borough have been fixed at ss. 3d. in the pound, which is regarded as most satisfactory in- deed. The rate is an increase of 3d. uponthe preceding year. The rates of St. Helens are is. 6d. less than the average for the Lancashire County boroughs.

'ST. HELENS TRADERS AND CO-OPERATION.

The St. Helens Private Traders' Defence Association met at the Fleece Hotel on Wednesday night, under the presidency of Councillor J. Green, J.P.—The Chairman referred at some length to the action brought by the Cowes Co-operative Society against the traders' official journal, and said they were proud and glad that the Co- operative Society had been worsted. They had begun to spend the £lOO,OOO defence fund they had boasted of, and if things went that way. they would soon make a hole in it. On local matters he said the St. Helens Co-operative Society has issued cards asking for boys and youths up to eighteen years of age, who had had some experience, for the grocery department. That meant that the boys trained by the tradesmen would go to the Co-operative Society for, perhaps, 2s. a week more,but when they came to an age to earn a proper wage they would have to fuake way for other boys or youths. Sr. HELENS FOOTBALL CLUB.

The annual meeting of the St. Helens Rugby Club was held on Thursday night, at the Town Hall. Mr. T. Garner presided over a fair attendance. The balance- sheet showeda total income of £l,llO 13s. 11d. made up of £932 18s. 9d. gate-money and transfer fees, subscrip- tions £ll2 15., &c., and a special donation of £2O from Captain Hughes, the president. For wages unpaid at the end- of last season £62 19s. has been paid, also £292 7s. 11d, wages and transfer fees, £3B 19s. I,ld. broken time, and £96 13s. 6d. for travelling expenses. This gives a total of £491 Os. 4d. expended on the players. All the accounts of the club have been paid, and £8 10s. remains in hand. The liabilities of the club are nil,and the assets are returned abb£l27 15s. the fixtures on the ground having been depreciated very considerably, although many improvements have been carried out. The accounts were passed. Mr. J. H. Houghton was re-elected secretary, and the officers and committee were appointed.

Postponement of the Council Meeting. The General Purposes Committee has decided that i in consequence of the day preceding the one fixed for the Council Meeting being a Bank Holiday, to post- pone the• Council Meeting from the 2nd to the 16th June next.

SIR JOHN BRUNNER AND THE PERILS OF PROTECTION.

Some Valuable Figures.

THE ENGLISH WORKMAN'S DELUSIVE

HOPE.

The annual meeting of Brunner, Mond, and Co., Limited, was held on Wednesday at the Law Association Rooms, Cook-street, Liverpool, Sir John Brunner, )LP., presiding.

The Chairman, in the first place, noted with regret the absence of Dr. Mond. He hadnot been at business for some months, but he (the chairman) was happy to say that his condition was steadily improving. He hoped that before long they would have Dr. Mond with them again, in all his wonted health. (Hear, hear.) With a note of very deep regret he had to refer to the loss the company had sustained in the death of Mr. Edward Milner, who had been a director of the company since its incorporation. He had never known a man so sweet- natured and so loving as Mr. Milner. He was an in- tense personal loss to everyone who had known him,and he had left behind him nothing but the sweetest and most fragrant of memories. earning to the accounts, Sir John said they showed the

CONTINUED PROSPERITY OF THE COMPANY. The gross profits were something like £48,000 more than last year. Last year the directors asked the share- holders to submit to a reduction of 2. per cent. in their dividends, and they asked the same request that day, in spite of the fact that their gross profit showed an increase of .C 48,000. The item of bad and doubtful debts stood, as usual, at a very satisfactory figure, and was exactly in consonance with their experience for twenty-two years, showing a marvellous amount of care in their commercial department. He had no doubt it made the mouths of a good many people water. He had to discuss the important matter of placing £125,000 to the reserve fund, which, if sanctioned, would raise the amount of the reserve fund to £850,000. He hoped that next year they would put an even larger sum to reserve, and should be pleased if they could raise the fund to

A ROUND MILLION. This policy had been adopted by many great English companies of late years. • It was a policy for conserving the funds of the company, and he considered ita very sound policy at all times, and more especially at the present time. There was a very great disturbance just now in the public mind as to the future commercial position of England, and fears—which to his mind were thoroughly well grounded—had been expressed as to what might happen to England if they changed their commercial policy. It there was no other reason than that reason he would strongly recommend the share- holders not to pay away all their profits, but to put 'something by for a possibly rainy day. To ils mind they as a manufacturing and business community were THREATENED WITH VERY GREAT DANGERS. The ideas held up before them were founded, as to one-half, upon imperfect information, and as to the other half upon wild anticipations far too hopeful. for ordinary business men to entertain. He was speaking of the proposals to change the commercial policy which this country had now maintained for fifty years. He noticed that the secretary of the Birmingham Gun Makers' Union had said that the proposals recently made would mean the making of the gun trade. If, with all their expenditure, they had not yet made the gun trade, he (Sir John) thought they might as well give it up as a bad job. The prospect was held out to the working classes of the country that if they multiplied the cost of their food and clothing by a considerable figure their wages would rise in proportion, and they would have a proportionate balance left over. He would give them some

ABSOLUTELY ACCURATE FIGURES bearing on this point. The wages paid in England were ! very considerably higher than those paid in all the highly Protectionist countries of Europe. For every hundred pounds or shillings received in wages by an English workman in a trade about which he(the chairman) knew everything, the wages in France were measured by the figure seventy-seven, and this was only earned by adaily work half as long again as the Englishman's. In Germany the figures of wages was seventy-eight, and , here again the workman had. to labour every thy half as long again as the Englishman. To his mind there was no hope:of raising wages by adopting a Protective policy, and he hoped that what he had said would be very care- fully considered and that the workpeople of this country would not be led into a policy which would raise the cost of their living in the wild and utterly, delusive hope that such a policy would raise their wages. From the employers' point of view he wished to speak as to the efficiency of the English workman under a system which induced the whole world to compete in supplying him with cheap food and cheap clothing. What was hicompara- tive efficiency as a producer? Again, he took the com- parative value at 100, and found that, probably in con- sequence of the low wages and the long hours, it took 124 Frenchmen and 131 Germans to do the same work as was done by 100 Englishman in a trade about which he (the chairman) knew everything. Consequently, neither from the point of view of the workman nor the employer was this proposed change in our fiscal arrange- -meats to be encouraged. He hoped all would join in DEPRECATING THIS PROPOSAL as likely to bring upon them as great a disaster us a coin- • mercial and manufacturing community could experience Sir John concluded by moving the adoption of the re- port, and a recommendation of the directors to the effect that a dividend of 7 per cent be paid to preference shareholders, and a dividend of 30 per cent. he paid to ordinary. shareholders.

Mr. Galloway seconded, and the resolution was adopted.

011 the motion of Mr. Holland, seconded by Mr. Henry Brunner, Messrs Jarmay, Vander Straeton-Solvay Robert Mond, and Alfred Mond were re-elected directors.

The directors were authorised to receive £1,200 in consideration of their services, on the motion of Mr. Webster, and by a further resolution Messrs T. W. Read and Co, were re-elected auditors at a renumera- tion of £725.

The Chairman moved a. vote of thanks to the staff,and paid a cordial tribute to their efficiency and active interest in the concern: They regretted the death during the year of Mr. Richards' who was for a long time manager of the Sandbach works. For the information of a certain shareholder who had asked the question, Sir John said that there med be no fear that 12 months hence the ordinary shareholders would be threatened with any further reduction of dividend. The prospects were all ol steadiness., (Hear, hear.) Mr. Galloway haNing seconded, the motion was agreed to. _ _

-The Chairman, in acknowledging a personal vote of thanks, reverted to the question of Protection. He remarked that the reason why this country had the most honest legislature in the world was because the legislators were nbt tempted. In every protected country in the world the people who desired Protection were able to OFFER BRIBES TO THEIR LEG ISIATIVE REPR ESENTIT IV ES, and these bribes were practically irresistible. How much out of a secret service fund could not a great manufacturing corporation in this country afford to offer to members of Parliament every year? At the time when he entered the alkali trade sulphur pyrites was obtained from Wexford and Wicklow. This, how- ever, was put out of the market by the Norwegian, which %MS stronger in sulphur and cheaper per unit. This also in turn was put out by Spanish, which, again, contained more sulphur and \las obtainable at a lower price. Supposing, under these circumstances, Mr. John Redmond in the House of Commons proposed that a heavy duty be put upon foreign pyrites in order to bring Trish pyrites into the market again. Brunner, Mond, and Co„ did not use any sulphur pyrites. If it went up in price their competitors would have to pay a highly advanced price for one of their principal raw materials. Would not that be an enormous gain to Brunner Mond ? They could very comfortably afford to spend ,£lOO,OOO in buying votes to carry out that proposal, and they would have every representative from Ireland voting in favour of Trebmd. (Cries of " Oh, oh," and laughter.) Tf they wanted to preserve the honesty of their legislature let them beware of import duties. (Hnar, hear.)

Shearer prosecuted and said prisoner was sent to gaol on February 13th on a similar charge. When he came out he com- menced work for Mr. Newton, contractor, at the wages of £1 per week. Due care may now mean a healthy and happy summer and autumn, and robust constitution to meet the trials of next winter. Neglect may now 'mean a joyless summer, a trying autumn, and a broken constitution to succumb under the attacks of any exceptional trial. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES. Police Change at Stockton Heath.

A WARRINGTON PUBLICAN PINED.

Thomas Broomhall Malkin, licensee of the "Red Lion " Inn Winwick-road, was summoned at the War- rington Po/ice Court, yesterday, for selling a quantity of whirkey which was not of the nature and quantity demanded by the purchaser, being 33 degrees under proof.—Mr. Stanley Wilsoa, deputy town clerk, prose- cuted and said that on Tuesday the 28th ult. Inspector Flood purchased through an agent a pint of whiskey which was supplied by the defendant. On being analysed it was found to be 33 degrees under proof, which was 8 degrees lower than allowed by law.— Defendant said he was quite ignorant of the fact that the whiskey was so much under proof. The gas was very near the jars of whiskey and must have caused the liquor to evaporate very quickly. His Inachinery was also defective.—A fine of 20s. and costs was imposed.

Police-Sergeant Bourne, who has been stationed at Stockton Heath for the past four years, is to be trans- ferred to Sale, and his place is to be taken by Sergeant Barlow, of Sale. The Mono-Railway. —New Company Registered. There was registered on Friday a company with a capital of 1,60,000, under the title of the Mono-Rail Construction Company, Limited (Behr's patents), whose principal object will be to make the issue of the capital required for the construction of the electric high-speed mono-railway between Manchester and Liverpool, as authorised by Parliament. The cost of i the building of the new line s estimated at £2,800,000 The entire capital of the company has been subscribed privately.

Warrington P.S.A. Mr. Sanderson presided at the meeting held in the Co-operative Hall. Sankey-street, on Sunday, Master T.Barton delighted the audience with excellen renderings of two violin solos, accompanied on the piano by Mr. Wadsworth. Mr. Holland gave an address on " The Reasonableness of Religion." An Old Bell at Sankey. Mr. John Gregson, of Penketh, near Warrington, writes to a Wigan contemporary with respect to ancient Wigan bells. He says :—" It may be of interest to your readers to know that the old bell recently taken down at St. Mary's Church, Great Sankey, near Warrington, bears the following name and date Luke Ashton, Wigan, 1728.' This would be about 12 years older than that recently purchased by the Mayor of Wigan. The hell is still on the premises, and I have no doubt could be seen by any- one wishing to do so."

A MILK DEALER'S OFFENCE-SMART FINE.

At the Warrington Police Court on Friday before Mr. H. Roberts and other magistrates, Thomas Bradley, Bath Spring Vaults, Waterloo-road, Widnes, was summoned for selling separated milk in a receptacle which did not bear a label with the wdrds "Skimmed milk " in large type.—Mr. Stanley Wilson, deputy-town Clerk, prose- cuted, and said that for some time the defendant had been in the habit of coming to Warringtonand retailing milk. On Thursday, April 23rd, Inspector Flood saw him in Hoyle-street with a pony and cart and he was crying out "Here you are. The old firm back again. Milk one penny a pint. Twopence a quart." On the following day one of the inspector's assistants went to defendant and asked him for a quart of new milk. Defendant replied. "It is separated milk," and was about to drive off when Inspector Flood called his attention to the fact that he had not complied with the law by having a proper label attached to the receptacle in which the milk was kept. He understood that since the proceedings had been instituted defendant had ceased coming to Warrington. —Defendant, who said there was a small label, was fined 20s. and costs.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
THEFTS BY A DOMESTIC SERVANT.

A domestic servant named Susan Kinsella, aged 15, Residing at 4, Halifax-street, was brought up at the Warrington Police Court on Saturday on the charge of stealing between May 16th and 22nd the sum of Hs. the money of Kate Bright.—The Chief Con- stable said that prisoner was in the service of Mrs. r idway, 35, Bewsey-street. The prosecutrix was a widow and had apartments at Mrs. Ridgway's. At ten o'clock on Saturday morning Mrs. Bright placed a satchel containing 10s. on the table in her bedroom and at four o'clock the same day she missed 2s. 6d. The matter was mentioned to Mrs. Ridgway and as the prisoner was the only person who went into the room suspicion naturally fell upon her. On the following Tuesday, Mrs. Bright sent the prisoner to the Post Office with a letter containing 18 penny stamps and not receiving any acknowledgement from the person to whom the letter was addressed she made inquiries and found it had never been delivered, and presumably had not been posted. At 6.30 on the following Thursday, Mrs. Bright placed three half-crowns ins box on the bedroom table, and at se.ven o'clock the next morning found they had all gone. Evidently the thief was getting bolder and the Detective Department was communicated with with the result that on Friday morning Superintendent Mona- ghan and Detective Barlow visited the house, and after making inquiries accused the girl of having committed the thefts. In a short time she was constrained to admit that she was guilty of the robberies. Prisoner's mother, who was well spoken of by a clergyman, was a very respectable woman and got her living by charring. Up to a year ago prisoner was in Padgate Industrial Schools. Shelwas not very strong and was really not fit to be in service. Prisoner's mother stated that the girl had been very good up to December last when she had an attack of influcutza.—On prisoner promising to go to a home the magistrates decided to adjourn the case sine die—Mr. H. Taylor, P.C.M„ promised to find the girl a suitable home.

Minor Cases. Warrington Borough Police Court.

FRI DA Y.—Before Mr. H. Roberts (presiding), Alderman Smethurst, Messrs. Hepherd and Thornton. CASE WITHDRAWN.—On the name of Mrs. Bowen, 7, Walter-street, being called, Mr. Taylor, solicitor (Messrs. H. Greenall and Company) explained that the charge was one of causing slight damage to a crop of oats in a field off Orford-lane, belonging to Mr. James Newton. Defendant kept a quantity of fowl which, in addition to the damage done by the woman herself, were constantly causing damage to the crop. Defend- ant would give an undertaking not to repeat the offence and he was willing to withdraw the case.—Mr. Steel represented the defendant, and said two dogs caused the hens to run on the field, and defendant went there to drive them off.—The case was withdrawn.

WIFE ASSA ULT.—Heim, Williams, 34 Atherton-street, was summoned for assaulting his wife Annie Williams on May 18th.—Complainant said defendant struck her with a bottle and kicked her. In the evening she went to bed, and defendant came upstairs, dragged her out of bed, and again kicked her.—He was fined 10s. and costs. WOMEN AT VAR lANCE .—Margaret Taylor,33 Beswick- street, was summoned for assaulting Margaret Skid- more, 25, Beswick-street, on May 19th.—Complainant, whose head was bandaged, said defendant commenced the unpleasantness by spitting in her face. Later on she struck witness on the head with a smoothing iron, rendering her unconscious.—Defendant said complain- ant first struck her with a poker,but complainant denied having done so.—Defendant was fined in the costs. NOTORIOUS CHARACTERS.—Patrick Richard Moran, 15 Portland-street, William Coyne, Bank-street, and Alfred Beckett, Friars' Green, were charged with having been drunk and disorderly on Friars' Green at 11.15 on Friday night.—P.C. Gilchrist proved the case.—The Chief Constable said Moran had been before the Court 19 times and Coyne had been in trouble repeatedly for theft, assault:ng the police, and other offences. The other prisoner was the celebrated, or rathernotorious— " Lawyer " Beckett, and he had been before the Bench 11 times. He had been inwrisoned for assaulting the police. Prisoners were three "regular young black- guards" and the town would be better for theirabsence for some considerable time.—Prisoners, who appeared to be greatly amused whilst their records were being read, were each sent to gaol for a month.

The Countess" and the Detective.

The applicants to.tha Stoke magistrates the ;other day it'd-tided the lady who is now well-known in a number of police courts, and Who describes herself as the " Countess of Stamford and Warrington." She said she was Mrs. William Gilroy, the Countess of Stamford and Warringt-m, and (handing the Bench a printed slip) her address Was printed on her poems. It appeared, she proceeded, that on the Ist of April a paper was found by her husband under the door of 19, St. Mary-de-Grace Cottages, Spring Gardens, Con- gleton, an untenanted house, addressed to Agnes Mary Gilroy." She was not "Agnes Mary G-ilroy," but Mrs. William Gilroy. It seemed that it was a paper sent by William Harris, who was described as a detective, of Stoke-on-Trent, whom she had never seen, and it stated that on the 3rd of March, in] the township of North Rode, this Agnes Mary Gilroy did unlawfully travel in a certain carriage on the N.S. Railway without having paid the fare, and with intent to avoid payment. That statement-was made before a magistrate.—The Magistrates' Clerk interrupted to ask what was the application. The court could only deal with an offence which arose in Stoke-on-Treat— Applicant: There was no offence. The " Countess " proceeded that she hatl been away from - hmae selling her poems, and at Manchester, Barton, and Eccles she had been taunted with going about impersonating the real person. Now she was the real person, and the application she wanted to make was really that of her husband, Mr. William Gilroy, who could not appear. She (reading from a paper) asked the magistrates to grant hini a free summons against William Harris, detective, of Stoke, in the county of Stafford.--The Mayor and the Bench refused the application.

OBJECTOR TO VACCINATION. Successful Application to the Warrington Magistrates. Mr. Michael George Hearne, of Alfred-street, Stock- ton Heath, made a successful application to Mr. F. Monks and other magistrates at the Warrington Police Court on Monday for an order exempting his child from vaccination. It will be remembered that Mr. Hearne made a similar application at the Daresbury Petty Sessions, but failed to convince the magistrates that he had a conscientious objection. The Chairman asked the applicant if he conscienti- ously objected to vaccination and received an answer in the affirmative. The applicant said he had gone into the matter more thoroughly since the Daresbury Petty Sessions, and was still more Convinced that vaccination would be prejudicial to the health of the child. Mr. S. Wright: You have a conscientious belief that it will be injurious. __

The Applicant Yes.- Very much so. Mr. S. Wright : That is quite sufficient. The application was granted.

CECIL RHODES _PROPHECY.

"One hundred years hence," said Cecil Rhodes the last time I met him, "when I look down from the sky at this little planet, I shall find that it has passed into the hands of a Hebrew financier."

It is a prophecy deserving to be classed with a similar confident prediction by a still more famous man, which has not been fulfilled. "In a hundred years," said Napoleon, "the world will either be Republican or Cossack." More than a hundred years have passed since Napoleon's prophezy, and although both the Cosgt.ck and the Republic have extended their sway over a con- siderable portion of the area of the planet, the world is still far from recognising the sovereignty of either, or even of both combined.

No one, not even Mr. Rhodes himself, were he still here, would-claim anything more for his forecast than that it summed up in a striking phrase the probable issue of the tendency of our times. the date fixed is not of the essence of the prophecy, and although more importance may be attached to the nationality of the future world-ruler, that also is a detail. The one essential point about the remark lies in its frank recog- nitionthat the sceptre of the world is passing from the hands of emperors, monarchs, soldiers, and politicians into those of the financier.

Money is the coming king, and the American dollar will be the emperor of the world. As the Egyptians had their dynasty of shepherd kings, so the whole wide world is to pass under the domination of money kings. Such, at least, was Mr. Rhodes' forecast, such the suggestion which has inspired the present series of studies of the Money Kings of the Modern World.— From an interesting article on "The Money Kings of the Modern World" by Mr. W. T. Stead in the June issue of the" Windsor Magazine," which is a splendid Midsummer number, including an elaborate survey of the Finsen Light Cure, and the wonders it is already azco nplishing ; a curiously picturesque article on " The Tame Fish of Logan ;" a long account of the renaissance of the Soudan, under the title of "The New Khartoum ;" and no less than seventeen favorite pictures from the popular brush of Mr. S. E. Waller, who incidentally contributes a number of amusing reminiscences of his career. A wealth of fic- tion includes Mr. S. R. Crockett's delighful new serial "Strong Mac," and capital stories by Spencer Leigh Hughes, Ethel Turner, Alick Munro, and Fred M. White besides the first of a new series of stories entitled' "Skin o' My Tooth," which promises that even the creator of" Sherlock Holmes "must look to his laurels. WOUNDS INFLICTED BY THE LEE-ENFIELD RIFLE.

Savage warfare again, and the same old cry of over- penetration with regard to the small-bore rifle! (writes Range finder" in "t'te Eingand His Navy and Army") I am almost sure that we labour under a false doctrine with regard to the pellet of the Lee-Enfield. Now the claim is that this weapon will not stop a man at short range. I believe this claim to be absolutely contrary to the facts. Speaking with the authority of one who was on Wagon Hill, I can assure you that at close ranges the usual effect of the small-bore, with its high muzzle velocity,is shattering at the point of exit.As a rule it is at medium ranges that the Mauser and Lee-Enfield make clean, penetrating wounds. When the pellet lum; taken effect at short range, many of the wounds at the point of exit are so severe as to suggest the expansive bullet. Hence the allegations which were made against us at Wagon Hill by the Boers that we were using Dum-Dum bullets and the accusations which we in turn mtde against them that they had "explosive' bullets. THE COST OF A MOTOR-BICYCLE.

It should be possible to put a motor-bicycle on the market for much less than £45. Where more is charged there is usually a considerable deviation from the standard pattern, and, of oourse, there are machines which cost less. One of the best of the foreign machines, sold in England at £45, is sold retail in the country of its origin at £39. There seems no good reason why this should cost £6 more in England. How- ever, I take the common price of £45 as the initial out- lay, although, as I say, the tendency will be for machines to become cheaper. The question of deprecia- tion presents itself first, though it will usually be found to be omitted in calculations like the present. A motor-bicycle of a good pattern and solid workman- ship, costing £45 new, ought, if it has been well cared for, to be saleable second-hand for £3O at the end of two years. Next, comes the expenditure upon petrol, depending, of course, upon the mileage covered. I take this latter at 10,900 miles in the year-200 miles a week—which will be greatly ex- ceeded during the fine month • and holidays, while for weeks in winter, in our delectable climate, the bicycle will never be taken off its stand. How many miles will a bicycle ran on one gallon ef petrol? The answer depends upon (1) the efficiency of the motor; (2) the skill of the driver,in not using a "mixture" (of air and petrol) wastefully. Eight out of sixteen makers say 100 miles, so I take this as a fair average,and put down the cost of petrol as £5 a year-10,000 miles at 100 miles per is. gallon. The question of the mileage-durability of tyres shows an even greater variety of opinion. On the whole I am inclined to think that a set of good tyres should run more than 15000 miles, and, therefore, / reckon the cost of tyres as I£6 a year, allowing for new treads. For supplies and renewals—lubricating accumulators (where the current is not produced from a magneto), &c.. after studying all the replies, I esti- mate at £3 a year, and repairs at £2 10s. The licence Costs 15s. On the other side of the account may properly be placed an obvious saving in 'bus, tram, and railway fares. £4 15s. a year will not be an extravagant estimate, and that brings the total annual cost of a first-class motor-bicycle, covering 10,000 miles, to precisely £2O. A motor-bicycle, therefore, averaging 200 miles a weekall the year round, involving an initial outlay of £45, and replaced by a new one every two years will cost its fortunate owner, for the present, a trifle over a shilling a day, or something less than a halfpenny a mile. It is incomparably the cheapest method of independent rapid locomotion since the world was oreated.—Mr. Henry Norman, M.P., in The World's Work " for May. THE CUCKOO'S USE.

Without leaving the currant bushes, the most casual rye can hardly miss the sight, at some time or other in summer, of slim, looping caterpillars, which look like parti-coloured black and whit 3 twigs. These are the caterpillars of the magpie moth, a conspicuous flimsy creature, with large white wings boldly spotted with black and yellow. Even the chrysalis is strikingly coloured, too, of glossy black with yellow bands. On the gooseberry bushes, too, you cannot help oftenseeing whole regiments of. shiny green-speckled grubs which flaunt their presence on the margins of the leaves, and cock up their tails with the menacealmost of scorpions when the branch is disturbed. These are the grubs of a sawfly, and very often whole rows of bushes will be stripped bare of leaves. As you might guess from the way in which both these grubs and the magpie caterpillars court observation they are not very palat- able to ordinary small birds; but where small birds are numerous the birds'-nesting cuckoo will surely come and a cuckoo among the fruit bushes is a glad sight for the gardener, because not the least mysterious feature of this mysterigus bird is its amazing appetite for nauseous grubs and very hairy caterpillars, which other birds cannot or will not eat. Thus from the clear-wing moth, which pretends to be a wasp to escape the small birds, to the cuckoo, which eats the grubs that have no fear of small birds, a single fruit bush provides anyone who has eyes to see with easy object-lessons of the chain of cause and effect in Nature—from which, when man drives out a link he suffers.—" The Garden." A STRANGE COINCIDENCE.

IWith a shiver we left Newgate for the sunny world outside. One of the party came up to us as we walked thoughtfully homeward, and said: "A good time ago, sir, I and my mate were coming towards Waterloo Bridge, up the steep hill on the Surrey side. A van had . been left by the driver; and the horse got frightened and bolted. It dashed right past me, and I made a rush , for it, but I was too late, and I couldn't catch it up the hill; and I thought there would be an awful smash in t the Strand. But. all of a sudden, a copper on Waterloo Bridge jumped in front of the horse, and managed to stop it at the risk of his life. I didn'tsay anything to him, but I strolled past (he was as white as a sheet) and took his number, and wrote to Scotland Yard. They came to my house and made inquiries about it, and I believe he was rewarded." The sp2aker turned and looked at us earnestly. "You saw that man's initial on that wall just now, sir; that cupper was Police Con- stable Cook, who was hanged a year later for a Murder committed on Wormwood Scrubbs. Queer, wasn't it ?"—From "Nisi Serenas," in the "Cornhill Magazine" for June.

THE BUILDING SOCIETY SENSATION. MR. PROUD COMMITTED TO THE QUARTER SESSIONS. ALLEGED HEAVY DEFALCATIONS. MR. H. ROBERTS AND THE AUDITORS' POSITION.

The full details of the case in which Mr. Robert Proud is charged with embezzling the funds of the War- rington Permanent Benefit Building Society were un- folded on Thursday when the accused iappeared before the magistrates at the Countyßorough Police Court for the third time. The great interest which has been a- roused by the prosecution was manifested by the large attendance of the public, the gallery being well filled. The first charge against the defendant was that on 24th April 1901, being the servant of the Warrington Per- manent Benefit Building Society he feloniously and I fraudulently did embezzle and steal £lOO and within six calendar months thereafter, namely on the 30th. July I 1901 a further sum of £4OO and on the 31st July 19010. further sum of £5O received by him on account of his emplc_lyers. Mr. Rigby Swift, barrister (instructed by 1 Mr. E. Draper, of Messrs Robert Davies and Co) prosecuted and Mr. A. Browne defended. The magis- trates on the bench were Mr. T. Birchall (presiding), Aldermen Smethurst and James Fairclough and Mr. Henry Roberts. I _ THE DETAILS OF THE CASE.

Mr. Swift said the Warrington Permanent Benefit Building Society was incorporated under the Building Societies Acts 1876 to 1894 and for about thirty years the defendant had acted as secretary. His duties were defined by rule 21 which provided thatalong with other duties the secretary or the solicitors should receive pur- chase or redemption moneys, and the secretary should receive subscriptions and other moneys payable to the society, enter moneys so received in the cash books with the name of the person paying such moneys, and forthwith pay the same to the society's bankers subject to such orders as the directors might from time to time make. The objects of the society were to receive subscriptions from its members and po advance the amount raised on mortgages to various depositors and mortgagees. Until the end of 1902 nothing appeared to have excited any suspicion on the part either of the directors or the auditors of the society. The Bench had nothing to do that day with the way in which theaudits were coaducted,but the fact remained whether the audit was good or whether it was bad—and there might be something to be said about that hereafter and in,another place—nothing wrong WAS discovered until last year. The society's financial year for 1902 ended on the 28th January, 1903, and at the end of the previous year—January 28th, 1902, the society had a debit balance at the bank of £52. When the accounts came out last January made up for the year 1902, the bank book showed a debit balance of about £448. That was, of course, a tremendous increase, and on February 25th when the accounts were being seen by the directors for the pur- pose of being made up for the year, the Chairmaz of directors asked Proud how they would stand when the accounts were fullymade up. Proud said : "Oh, about the same as last year." The Chairman said: How can that be having regard to the fact that we are now £448 out, whereas last year we Were only £52 out ?" Proud replied that that would be "all right." When the final draft balance sheet was prepared it appeared to have been made all right, or comparatively all right, because the debit balance had been turned into a credit balance of £99 Sc. Bd. Therefore a, considerable sum appeared to have been paid into the bank after the Chairman's comment as "to how it would be possible that the balance would be the same. The Chairman: Had the balance sheet been issued then?

MT. Swift: No, nor has it, in fact, ever been issued. The Chairman: It was prepared, I think. Mr. Swift: It was prepared by Proud. It was also printed and audited, and that showed the sum of £99 3s.' Bd. to the credit of the society. Alderman Smethurst: Instead of a debit balance you ge_t_a credit. _ _

- Mr. Swift, continuing, said inquiries were made as to how that had been done, and it appeared from the last entry in the bank pass book on March 14th, 1903, there was an entry in Proud's handwriting of cash, £440, making it appear that that money had been paid into the bank. As a matter of fact it had not been paid into the bank. It was paid into the bank afterwards, when proceedings were about to be taken, but it was not paid into the bank on March 14th, and there was no entry of it, except by Proud. Suspicion having been aroused in that way, the society appointed two independent audi- tors—Ntr. Bradshaw and Mr. Woodcock—to go through the society's books. The examination proved to them that the accounts were short to the extent of £2,649 10s. sd. The irregularities in the books were innumer- able,and it- appeared that for at least the last ten years, which was as far back as the auditors had gone, there had been defalcations in the cash, which had been covered over by fals:i ia the books. The audi- tors had discovered some 29 instances of money having been abstracted. So far as to such wholesale frauds which had taken place n so many different ways Mr. Proud appeared to have worked on five different prin- ciples. an the first place he had not accounted for money of the society he had received to pay off mort- gages, but had apparently pocketed it. At any rate he had not accounted for the money. Another system he had adopted was that of debiting mortgagors with advances which they had not received, and crediting himself in the cash book as having paid those advances. For example he would make an entry in the ledger against a mortgagor's name as having advanced him £lOO. He would also make an entry in the cash book stating that he had paid that £lOO. As a matter of fact he had not advanced the money but had put himself in control of it. Another system he had adopted was that having received a deposit he did not enter it in the cash book at all, but entered it in the depositors' ledger 'and then cleared it off again by making a fictitious entry of a transfer As an illustration, he received £4OO, did not put it in the cash book, but apparently into his-- pocket. He entered that amount on the credit side of the depositors' ledger, and in order to get rid of that amount he made a fictitious entry of a transfer on the debit side. He by that means sqyored off the ledger, so that the auditors passed it over as having been squared off, whereas he ' had pocketed the money. Another system was that of paying expenses out of the oash which he had in hand and drawing a cheque for the Same amount, so that apparently the sante amount had been paid twice over. The difference was that he did not put the cheque into his cash book, which enabled him to pocket the amount for which the cheque was drawn. Another example of his systems was to enter into his cash books amounts alleged to have been paid by him to creditors, whichhe hadnever paid at all. Those were systems the auditors had discovered, and what he (Mr. Swift) proposed to do was to call evidence giving an example of each kind. It was, of course, impossible for that Court or for any other Court to take cognizance of the• whole of the 29 defalcations and the whole of the false entries which had been made, but he proposed to select one example of each of the cases he had explained, and he would ask the Bench to commit Proud to the next Liverpool Assizes on charges of embezzlement and falsification of accounts.

ASSIZES OR QUARTER SESSIONS?. After consulting with Mr. Browne, Mr. Swift said that Mr. Browne suggested that the case should go to the Quarter Sessions. It was, of course, entirely a matter for the justices as to whether they would send the case to the Assizes or to the Sessions. The prosecutors were quite indifferent. They had nothing to do except to lay the facts before them, and leave the Bench to exer- cise their discretion. At the same time he ventured to suggest that a case of this seriousness and magnitude was properly tried at the. Assizes and not at the Quarter Sessions. He confessed that during his experience he never knew of a case of such magnitude and import- ance to be tried at the Quarter Sessions, but if the justices thought it right and proper that the case should go to the Sessions the prosecutors would he was going to say, cheerfully submit. They would submit because they had no voice in the matter.

fliE ALLEGED FALSIFICATION OF BOOKS. Mr. Swift proceeded to state that the first case he would briar, before the notice of the Bench had refer- ence to the falsifying of the depositors' ledger. Mr. Browne said that Mr. White (the magistrates clerk) had not read out the charge of allegedfalsifloa- tion of books and therefore his learned friend was not entitled to go into that matter.

Mr. Swift- said his friend was quite wrong in his law. He contended that they could go on without that charge being read, as their worships, having heard what he would bring forward, could decide on what offences they would send the defendant for trial.

The Chairman : Why not iet-ih;dlerk read the second indictment?

Mr. Swift: You have nothing to do with the indict- ments. You have only to deal with the evidence and say whether that is sufficient to send him for trial. It is absurd to say that the evidence on each particular matter must be separate. it is never done. At any rate I take the responsibility. Mr. Browne: He knows that the responsibility would not be upon him, but upon yourselves. Mr. H. Roberts: What is your remedy? Mr. Browne: To apply for a mandamus. Mr. Roberts: That is a mandamus to re-hear the case.

Mr. Swift said he was doing his best to save the time of the Bench. He was quite willing, although he did not agree with Mr. Browne's view, to have the second indictment read.

The Clerk then charged Mr. Proud with unlawfully and wilfully making false entries in the books of the society.

TYPICAL CASES.

Proceeding, Mr. Swift said he proposed to deal specifically with the five different methods which the prisoner had' adopted. The first one was a case of falsifying the depositors' ledger. On the 18th of July, 1900, it appeared from the depositors' ledger that the society owed a Mr. Erlam £4OO. Mr. Erlam died on the 23rd April, 1900, and Mr. Swain became the execu- tor, along with the wife, who had since died and left Mr. Swain sole executor. On the 30th. July, 1901, Mr.

Proud entered the sum of £4OO to the debit of Erlam as having been transferred to folio 51 of the Journal. Mr. Henry Roberts: By that means it appeared as though Erlam's account had been closed? Mr. Swift: As a matter of fact the account was not transferred as the books led one to believe, and that was a fictitious entry. It might be as you suggest that Ihe ought to have closed the account on the death of Mr. lErlam but he did not close it on that account. He closed it in order to make this transfer which was a false one. He did not make the transfer and pay off the £4OO to the executor but continued to pay dividends I upon it as though the account was still open. In 1900 and 1901, he paid Mr. Swain dividends on the £4OO, and Mr. Swain continued thinking that his account was still going, although the auditors thought it was closed. In order to wipe off the £4OO he opened an account in Swain's name for two sums of £lO each, so that the total effect of the manipulations in that instance left in Mr. Proud's hands £3BO. No doubt when he opened these two fresh accounts he thought it would cover up the defalcations. That was an example of his making a false transfer in the depositors' ledger. He (Mr. Swift) would now give an example of a re- advance being entered in the .mortgage ledger which never in faqt took place. A Mrs. Travis, of Douglas, Isle of Man had a mortgage for £4OO on January 31st, 1900. Thus, on that date, she owed the society £4OO. On September 26th, 1900, she repaid £lOO of that £4OO, and that was entered in the ledger. Defendant subse- 9uently entered the £lOO as being re-advanced, and instead of putting any year against it as showing the date of the re-advancement he simply put a dot, which constantly occurred throughout the false entries. In the cash book under date April 24th, 1901, he showed the re-advance. Therefore, his booksappeared as though he had paid Mrs. Travis £lOO, although she had never had it. He (Mr. Swift) now proposed to give an ex- ample of where credit had not been given when a mortgage had been paid off. He would call attention to the case of a Mr. Silcock. On December 18th, 1901, Mr. Silcock paid £142 9s. Bd. to Messrs. Robert Davies and Co., the society's solicitors. They paid the amount into the bank, and at the same time sent notice of the payment to Mr. Proud. He ought to have entered on the debit side of his cash book £142 9s. Bd. as cash re- oeived, and the same on the other side as paid into the bank. Instead of that he entered on the debit side only £92 9s. Bd. and on the credit side £142 9s. Bd. By that means Pe obtained £5O, which would no doubt be used by him to cover up the defalcations. Proceeding Mr. Swift gave an example of Mr. Proud's system of paying expenses out of his cash and drawing a cheque for the same amount. On January 28th, 1903, according to the cash book, he paid in fees for auditors and directors, &c., £53 155., and at the same time he drew a cheque which he got signed by the directors for the same amount, so that it appeared as though they were paid twice. They were not paid twice and Proud himself pocketed £53 15s. The last example he pro- posed to give was that of entering into the cash book ' amounts alleged to have been paid by him to creditors, which he had never paid at all. On the 28th of January he entered in his cheque book £152 7s. 4d. as having been paid to the Widnes Corporation. Mr. Proud had admitted since that that sum had never been paid. Mr. Roberts: Is that an item which has been examined by the auditors? Mr. Swift: It has been examined by the special auditors.

Mr. Roberts,: Was it before the last balance sheet waslissued ?

Mr. Swift: Yes. It was on the last day of the society's year and would be before the annual audit. Mr. Roberts: Them it would be included in the figures of the annual audit, and the auditors it would seem, have passed a debit for which there was no receipt. Mr. Swift said they had passed much worse things than that.

Mr. Robert: I should hardly think they could pass anything much worse than that. Mr. Swift: Perhaps not worse but equally bad. THE LATE CHAIRMAN'S EVIDENCE.

THE CASE OF MR. SWAIN.

Newton Swain grocer, Liverpool-road, Warrington, stated that the late Mr. John Erlam, of 1, Thewlis- street,Warrington, by his will appointed his wife and witness and executrix and executor. Mr. Erlam died on April 23rd,1900, and the will was proved. Mrs. Erlam died in March, 1901, having appointed witness sole executor of the will which witness subsequently proved At the time of his death Mr. Erlam was a depositor in the society to the amount of £4QO but witness had never received any payment on account of the principal of money deposited by Mr. Edam. He had received two dividends in 1900 and 1901. In the year 1900 the amount deposited in the Society was under notice of withdrawal and in consequence witness only received £2 10s. as dividend. He afterwards withdrew the notice of withdrawal and in 1901 received a full dividend of £lO. Witness did not in 1902 deposit anything in his own name and he did not in that year authorise the de- fendant to open an account in his name. He did not

PASSIVE RESISTANCE.

The Obnoxious Education Act,

I The Rev. J. F. Matthews has addressed the following letter to Mr. W. H. Woodcock, the Borough Collector, refusing to pay the rate levied under the new Education Act.

Latchford Baptist Church,Warrington,22nd May,1903. My dear Mr. Woodcock, I have received my demand notes for the rates, and in due course shall pay them as usual save that portion of the rate for Education, which I believe in my case amounts to ss. for the half-year. I do not know whether the rating authorities have in- chided the Education rate in the Borough rate, with the view of outwitting those of us who have adopted the ' passive resistance attitude, but if that is the case they will be defeated in their object. I need scarcely tell you how obnoxious the present Education Act is, and how it has been distinctly framed in the interests of the national church and with a view of making educa-,I tion subserve her interests. Can you imagine anything more likely to arouse the antagonism of the country, than rating Nonconformists for the support of schools in which such arch-doctrines of Anglicanism and Romanism are taught as Baptismal regeneration,the in- I tervention of a priest between the human soul and God, and the awful, and to us wicked doctrine, of the right and power of the same person to grant absolution and remission of sins, claimed by priests of both churches and taught in the schools. What would you think of a Baptist minister paying towards such propaganda'! I never intend to contribute towards the obsequies of Nonconformity, even in Warrington nor do I even intend being present at its funeral. I fear therefore you will have to ta.kejthe only course that remains for securing that portion of the rate I refuse to pay.—Ever sincerely yours, JOHN FRANCIS MATTHEWS. Minister.

M. W. H. Woodcock

===== 0002601_19030529_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VOLUNTEER CAMP IN MANXLAND. The Ground Inspected.

Last week-end the Quarter-Masters and other officers of the 30th Field Army Brigade, which includes the Ist V.B. Prince of Wales's Volunteers, South Lancashire Regiment, visited the Isle of Man for the purpose of inspecting the ground where this year's encampment will take place. Needless to say the Warrington volun- teers are looking forward with feelings of pleasure to the forthcoming visit to the picturesque island. After the rigours of camp life on Salisbury Plains the change in the venue of the battalion will be welcomed, and the encampment which will extend from July 25th to August Bth should be one of the most successful yet experienced. The officers who made the inspection last week-end were unanitnous in the opinion that the ground is admirably adapted for military purposes. It is situated at Lewaigue about two miles outside Ramsey, and com- prises about 50 acres of grass and hayfields—dry and level. Water from two mountain streams runs on three sides of the ground and these the Ramsey Town Com- missioners propose to drain, and make two reservoirs with filtering beds, and lay the water free of expense on to the ground direct, furnish- ing three stand pipes to each battalion. The fourth 1 side of the camp is bounded, in fact touched, by the 1 electric railway which runs from Ramsey to Douglas and a siding for passengers and goods will be con- 1 structed on to the camp ground. Low fares have been I arranged for the men in camp wishing to visit Douglas or Ramsey. Thiselectric railway is so convenient that 1 the Ramsey Commissioners have agreed with the Manx. 1 Electric Railway Company to light the whole of the camp, if necessary, with electric light. All lights would of course, be switched off at the proper time. The troops will have the opportunity of indulging in good sea bathing in a small bay within five minutes' walk from the camp. The manceuvring area is close at hand, and comprises many square miles of ground with facilities for field firing. The roads to the area are manyand good. There are excellent sources for supplies of food and necessaries available in Ramsey for the satisfactory working of the camp commissariat. There will be a post-office and telephone on the ground, in fact everything possible is being done for the comfort and convenience of the Volunteers. The arrangements for the passage, &c.have been practically completed.

WARRINGTON.

Licensing. At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Fredrick Clare, licensee of the King and Queen Hotel, Padgate- lane, applied for a music licencelt was stated that the applicant only wanted the licence to enable an Odd- fellows' club which met at the. hotel to have social evenings, &c. His mother, previous to her death, had a music licence—The application was granted. The Nursing Association.

Warringtonians had an opportunity of supporting this deserving institution on Saturday when a. street collection organized by the Warrington and District Christian Endeavour Union was made. -The amount realized was about 30 and the members of the Union are to be congratulated upon the results of • their efforts. Victory Celebrated.

The members of the Thelwall Football Club and their friend S had a tea and social evening in Thel- wall Schools on Saturday to celebrate the victory gained over Oughtrington Park in the final tie for the Warrington Cup. Mr. Joseph Wright was the caterer The tables and the rooms were tastefully decorated with plants and flowers. After tea the tables were cleared away, and the remainder of the evening was spent very enjoyably in songs, glees, dances and speeches. Mr. H. Roebuck presided and referred to the loyalty and good spirit shown by the members during the past season, and ventured to prophesy that if they continued to work together next season as they. had done .this they would stand a very good chance of winning the clip again.—Messrs. Harvey, Bradshaw and Forbes, representing the Warrington Association, were present.-Mr. Harvey said that no d utbt the best team had won, and he congratulated Thelwall on their victory. The Warrington Association was in a better financial position that it had even been, and there was a god prospect for the future.—Mr. Brad- shaw said it was proposed next season to have a Works' Competition, which, he thought, would in- crease the interest taken in the Warrington Football Association. Votes of thanks were accorded to the ladies, who had taken part in the entertainment, and Mr. Roebuck for his services as chairman Lifeboat Saturday.

There is to be a fifeboat Saturday demonstration at Warrington on September sth. The General Com- mittee met at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening under the presidency of Mr. D. Davies to discuss the preliminary arrangements. Mr. Johnson attended on behalf of the Royal National. Lifeboat Institution. After some discassion it was decided to hold the demonstration on September sth. There will be a parade through the streets, a fancy dress ball in Parr Hall in the evening, and a smoking concert in the Gymnasium. It was decided that in addition to the street collections, there should also be collections at the various works, shops and offices in the town, and a request is to be made to the clergy and minis- ters to have collections taken at the various places of worship.—lt was agreed to have a lifeboat and crew in the procession, and Mr. Johnson suggested that it should either be that from Southport or the one from Hoylake.—The cost of bringing the boat to Warring- ton and the payment of the crew, it was stated, would be from to 115.—Some discussion took place as to the best means of getting the lifeboat here, and Captain Waring promised to see what could be done with a view to getting the Ship Canal Company to bring it down the Ship Canal.—The question of launching the lifeboat was considered to be impractic- able, as no suitable place could be secured for the purpose. --The Ambulance Corps, and the Fire Brigade will also be asked to take part. The tradesmen are invited to make a display of turnouts. In addition, 'there will be a fancy dress cycle parade, and prizes will be given for the best" get-ups."-TA further sug- gestion was made that there should be a sacred con- cert in Parr Hall on the Sunday night, but on the motion of Mr. Steel, it was decided not to hold one.— The name of Mr. J. Brooks, Darlington Lodge, was added to the General Commtttee, together with the district secretaries and chief officers •of the various friendly societies in the town.

The Examiner, Friday, May 29, 1903. THE FREE CHURCHES. SUICIDE OF MR. T. B. MALKIN.

The many Warrington friends of Mr. T. .B. Malkin heard with painful surprise on Monday morning that he had committed suicide by hanging himself from a beam in his back kitchen by means of a silk muffler tied round his neck. Latterly Mr. Malkin had been the licensee of the Red Lion Inn, Winwick-road, whither he had removed from the Royal Exchange Inn, Market-place. On Friday last week, Mr. Malkin was fined 20s. and costs for selling whisky under proof and while the case was pending and since the hearing he had been very despondent, but no one anticipated that he would take away his life. However, on Monday morning shortly before eight o'clock he was discovered hanging by a silk muffler attached to a hook in the beam of the kitchen. A man named Arthur Skidmore of 88, Plumpton-street, cat the body down and Dr. Curtis was summoned, but it was found that life had been extinct for some time.

IMr. Malkin to Warrington to take up employ ment at Bewsey Forge as an ironworker. He early Ibegan to take an interest in Trades Union matters and was elected as a representative of the ironworkers on I the Warrington Trades and Labour Council. Here he I took part in the discussions on matters affecting labour and was ultimately elected president of the Trades and !Labour Council. During the protracted coal strike of I ten years ago he was a useful member of the Relief Committee. For several years he was a member of the Board of Guardians. In 1899 he made an effort Ito become a member of the Town Council, and con- tested Bewsey Ward against Cciuncillor Rogers. The result was a tie, and on the election being re-fought, Councillor Rogers gained a victory by twelve votes. In the following year Mr. Malkin was returned to the Council unopposed, and last November when his term 'of office expired, he did not seek re-election. Since I then he took no part in local affairs. THE INQUEST. I__ _ _

Mr. S. Brighouse conducted the inquest at the Police Stationjon Tuesday evening. James Malkin, son of the deceased, said his father was 54, years of age and was the licenoee of the Red Lyon Inn, Winwick Road. Deceased went to bed at eleven o'clock on Sunday night as usual and at 8 o'clock on Monday morning witness came downstairs and on going into the back kitchen found him hanging by a silk muffler attached to a hook in the beam. He called for assistance and a man named Skidmore who was in the yard came and cut the body down. Dr. Curtis was summoned and said deceased had been dead for some considerable time. On Friday deceased was fined for selling adulterated whisky. While theca.se was pending and since the trial deceased had been very low spirited but witness had never heard him threaten to take his life. The Coroner: Was there no other trouble? Witness: No. The Coroner: Do you mean to say a small thing like that worried him?

Witness : It worried him a lot before the trial came off?

_ The Coroner: What small matters affect some people's minds! The Coroner said it might seem foolish that a small thing like that should worry anybody, but all people were differently constituted. Some people got upset by small things which would not affect others. It might have seemed a grave thing to the deceased to be brought before the magistrates. The jury "returned a verdict of " Suicide whilst of unsound mind," and expressed their sympathy with Mrs. Malkin and family.
