THE RESIGNATION OF MR. F. MONKS.

LETTER TO THE MEMBERS OF THE EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

The Opposition of the Non-Traders.

Mr. F. Monks has addressed the following letter to the members of the Education Committee :—" Dear Sir,—As several members of the Education Committee have appealed to me not to resign the honourable posi- tion of Chairman of the Committee I think it only fair to give you my reasons why I cannot accede to their request. I had hoped that the methods of the Non-Traders' Association would at least have been kept from intrud- ing into the domain of the new Education Committee but Monday's experience proves to the contrary; and Mr. Dodds' opposition, supported by so many members of the Committee, who are also members of Town Conn- cil,leads me to the conclusion that there is a deliberate attempt, partly on personal ground, but mainly on the plea of economy, to retard the work that has been done for Education, and also the work contempla- ted. The threat that if the proposals of the Committee were passed they would be opposed in the Town Coun- cil, destroys the freedom of our. action and its policy, and proceedings must be guided not by what the Com- mittee considers most economical, but by what Mr. Dodds and those who support him in the Town Council will permit. The plea of economy will always be a popular cry, but what is economy? The economy that is foreshadowed by the opposition is no economy, but will end in disaster, be more costly, with inefficient administration, and discredit to all concerned. The men who now oppose will be the first to turn round upon the Committee and blame them for every little fault, if not with blundering and plundering. The difference between the proposals of the Sub- Committee, and the resolution unanimously adopted in- creasing the salary of the director by £25 a year, cannot be considered economy if it leads to changes in them- selves costly, delay to organization, and the risk of losing an efficient officer. To be consistent you would have to offer to a new man a salary of £325 for the duties Mr. Murray has undertaken. Will such an offer bring you the man you require, when other towns are offering so much more? It may or may not, but under any circumstances the difference in salaries is gone in expenses and delays, and you incur the risk of not getting the most efficient service. I simply mention this as an illustration of what is called economy. I fail to see where economy comes in, but Ido see a waste of public money. My experience of well nigh 50 years in business has taught me that it is far better to spend freely and make a profit, than to spend with a niggaray hand which lands you in perpetual difficulties if not in bankruptcy. Then again, lam sure the com- mittee does not realize the enormous amount of work the organization of the 21 schools with their 13,586 children really m,eans to the staff, and this leads to a fundamental diffeince of opinion in the conception of the work to be done and the remuneration to be paid to the staff who will have the responsibility of success or failure. The cleverest man in the world with an ineffi- cient staff will be compelled to give up, not because he cannot do the work but because as a professional man he cannot risk failure from causes which are beyond his control.

It is because I see very clearly that such a policy will lead to disaster, and make the work of the Committee an intolerable burden to those who will have to-do the work and bear the responsibility, that I decline to be a party to it. Under the most favourable circumstances with every loyal sup- port the Committee can give, it will be a most difficult problem to work out, requiring great tact and judgment in reconciling the conflicting interests that will come under the central authority for the first time. The opposition speak as though it will be an easy thing showing how little they understand the nature and ex- tent of the work in which they, as yet, have taken little or no part. Success and failure are not the work of a day, ani a policy can only be judged by its results. If I feel convinced in my own mind that the Committee are being led into a difficult position, one that will end in failing to secure the best results in Education more costly administration, inefficient management of depart- mental work, and that has in it the seeds of corruption, it is my duty to the Town Council,and the public whose servant Tam, to resign my position as Chairman of the Committee. .

Ihave no personal ends to serve, I am anxious that the work of the Committee shall be well and efficiently done, and it is because I am confident that a cheese- paring policy will not attain this result, that I prefer to retire altogether. Neither do I care to place myself in a position of responsibility where I may be unfairly attacked for the results of a policy my own judgment does not approve.—Yours faithfully, F. MONKS.

Walton Old Hall, 20th May, 1903.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
A Request to Re-consider. The ,following lettar was addressei to Mr. Monks on Friday:— , .

Warrington, May 22nd, 1903.

Dear Mr. Monks,—We, the undersiined Co-opted members of the Education Committee, have received with great regret your letter of the 20th inst.,in which you announce your determination to resign the Chair- manship. We desire to express our sincere sympathy with you under the painful circumstances of the personal attack made upon you at the meeting of the 18th inst., and we deprecate the tone which characterized the introduction of the amendment.

We entirely agree with you that "the threat that if the proposals of the Committee were passed they would be opposed in the Town Council, destroys the freedom of our action," and that it is prejudicial to the best interests of Education: moreover weare convinced that the policy which you have advocated in the organization of the Education in this Borough deserves and will receive from us the heartiest approval and support. We earnestly trust that, even now, you will reconsider your decision, and that the opinion of your fellow co-opted members will have peculiar weight with you. With every expression of respect, for yourself person- ally and, for all the good work you have done in th 9 past.—We are, dear Mr. Monks, yours very faithfully, W. BIBBY, J. FULTON SMITH, R. T. FAIRCLOUGH, MORLEY STEVENSON, E. J. GUEST, GEORGE WALKER, A. J. JOLLEY, E. J._WILLoOCKS,

T. MAY,

JOHN YONGE,

===== 0002601_19030529_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
Walking Day.

The Sunday School .officials are already making preparations for Walking Day, which will be beld on Friday, July 3rd. This is in accordance with the rule laid down of holding this annual children's festi- val on the nearest Friday to the first of July. The children are already eagerly looking forward to the event., The summer holidays in the elementary schools will not commence until July 17th so that only one day's holiday will be given br Walking Day. The New Railway Goods Manager.

The valuable services which Mr. I. T. Williams,the newly-appointed London and North-Western Railway goods . manager fdr the Warrington District, has rendered as hon. treasurer of the Abergavenny Work- men's Hospital Saturday Fund have recently been fittingly recognized. Mr. Williams was presented with an address from the members expressing their high appreciation of his work on behalf of the cause. The followind is an extract from the :address "Whenever called upon in this labour of love you have always cheerfully responded, and have aided the committee in every •possible way with your great talents and abilities, until our fund has been thoroughly established amongst the working people of Aberga- venny. We deeply regret that we are called upon to part from you, but our best wishes for your welfare will go with you to the greater sphere of usefulness you are called upon to fill." Several eulogistic speeches were made, and Mr. Williams was congratu- lated upon his appointment to Warrington.—Mr. I. T. Williams suitably replied, stating that it was with the deepest gratitude he accepted their handsome present.. —Mr. Williams was subsequently entertained to a farewell dinner by a number of his personal friends, who presented him with a handsome silver salver and silver tea and coffee sets.— In acknowledging the gift, Mr. Williams said he could assure them his promotion was very greatly enhanced by the knowledge that it had given so much pleasure to his numerous friends, whose kindness enabled him to go away with credentials of which any man might feel most proud. They would, he knew, be pleased to hear that in thiS new home, in Lancashire, he had been given the most cordial welcome, and had already felt that he was amongst those who were so well- known for their warm heartedness and sincerity. It I was for them to add to his enjoyment when going north, by remembering that nothing would give him greater pleasure than to see his old friends when they had the opportunity of calling. (Applause). He would treasure that valuable service of plate so long as be lived, and after hi S time it would, he trusted, go to one who was born in Abergavenny, one who was known to all of them, and by whom, he was quite sure it would be regarded as a token of the great friend- ship he had enjoyed, and of the many happy years he had spent in their midst.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CONDITION OF THE MINISTRY.

Considerable criticism has been aroused by the discussion on "The Condition of the Ministry" at the Spring Meetings of the Baptist Union. The Rev. J. G. Greenhough, M.A., who introduced the subject, drew to some extent a startling picture of the condition of the ministry in his own dencmination. He held that the present supply of ministers is greater than the demand, that the competition for pastorates is bitterly keen, and that many ministers are living on a starvation stipend. He suggested that the output of ministers should be restricted; that the colleges should lengthen the average term from three and a half years to five at least, and so, though keeping as many students in training as before, limit the output by one-third. A writer in the Free Church Chronicle obtained an inter- view with the Rev. G. P. Gould, M.A., on this subject, and gives Mr. Gould's comments in the June number of that magazine. The Principal of Regent's-park Baptist College holds that it is not so much a question of the output of the colleges, but of the large number of ministers who have not been to college at all. "If we limit the number of men whom we train, it does not necessarily follow that the number of men who find their way into the ministry will be lessened; there will be a smaller number of trained men in the ministry." Of course, these problems are peculiar to the Congregational and Baptist bodies. In other denominations the matter takes a different turn; the Wes- leyans, for instance, are finding they have a scarcity of ministers, and students are being hurried through their course to take their places in the circuits. The scarcity of curates in the Anglican church has become notorious.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EDUCATION FIGHT.

The hostility called forth by the educational policy of the Government shows no sign of abatement. In addition to the huge Hyde- park Demonstration, in which the Metropolitan Free Church Federation co-operated with the National Labour Education League and the Progressive bodies against the London Bill, the National Free Church Council is continuing its fight against the administration of the Act of last year. By means of the local Councils, with which the whole of England and Wales is covered, it is collecting information as to the composition of Education Committees, local authorities, religious education in non-provided schools, and the whole operation of the Act. The Board of Education supplies the National Council with copies of 'all draft, interim, and final orders issued, and particulars as to these can be obtained by application through the local Councils.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
"CAPTURE THE BOARD SCHOOLS!"

The Education Act of 1902 does not, it seems, represent to any extent the final desires and expectations of the Clerical Party. Now that the Denominational Schools have been foisted upon the rates an attempt will now be made to "capture the Board Schools," or rather Council Schools, as they will now be called. A branch of the "London Diocesan Board of Education" thus explains one of its objects: "To oreanise a scheme for Church Teaching in State Elementary Schools, if permitted." Free Churchmen must certainly be on the alert to see that this is not "permitted."

===== 0002601_19030529_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GIRLS' GUILD MOVEMENT.

Although the Free Church Councils have during the last year been absorbed almcrt en. tirely in the education fight, another vr ,rk has been quietly but very rapidly growing ;LI con. nection with the Federation Moveme, t. The Girls' Guild scheme, although only in•tagurated a few years ago, has now been taken up in many places all over the country. At Maid- stone, for instance, there is a flourishing Guild, which is in possession of a permanent home for meetings, socials, Sz-4. Shorthand, district nurses, dresscutting, and other classes are held with manifest success. The influence of the Guild on girl life is most marked, and it is felt that as the work could not be undertaken by individual churches or organisations it should have the loyal support of Free Churchmen and women in general.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
LABOUR AND THE FREE CHURCHES.

It is not generally known that Mr. Hodge, the Labour candidate who greatly reduced the Tory majority at the Preston election, is a pro- flounced Free Churchman. For some years past he has regularly attended the Victoria Wes- leyan Church, Manchester, and of ten appears as a speaker at the men's Bible Class.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
NONCONFORMIST MUSIC.

Nonconformist choirmasters and choirs are looking forward to the Annual Festival of the Nonconformist Choir Union which will be held at the Crystal Palace on June 13. Mr. E. Minshall, who is the president and conductor, is well known in Free Church circles, hating been for many years organist of the City Temple, London. Mr. Minshall contributes an open letter to choir leaders to this month's Free Churchman. He thinks that much could be done to raise the standard of Nonconformist church music, and his admirable suggestions will certainly be read with interest by those who are responsible for the music in our churches.

===== 0002601_19030529_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. ROBERTSON NICOLL.

The Rev. W. Robertson Nicoll, M.A., LL.D., the editor of the British Weekly, is looked upon as the backbone of the Passive Resistance movement. Dr. . Nicoll is one of, if not the, ablest among journalists on the religious Press. Indeed, he has been described as "the Prince of Editors." A paper which he contributes to this month's Free Clwrchman gives an explana- tion to his success and an insight into his methods of working. He writes on " How to Become a Journalist," and his article has the merit of being very practical. "I do not dis- guise from you," be says, "that it takes rare qualities to make and maintain a success in journalism. It means quickness, alertness of faculty, unsparing industry, and the most rigid accuracy and punctuality."

FREE CHURCH FEDERATION.

The net-work of organisations with which the country is covered in the interests of the Free Church movement has been added to by the formation of a Federation for Suffolk. At a conference recently held representatives were present from all the Councils in the county, and the Rev. T. J. Hoskens, of Ipswich, was chosen first president.

It is fa.tened on the inside, and I must olimh over." This remark was made by a man who was standing at a gate on a lonely road, and it wee addressed to a stalwart sailor who had just ecene up. A Young Man's Downfall.

The case of George Scully (21), of Bradford-road, Manchester, is a sad one. He stole .V.OO from his employers, Messrs. Armitage and Rigby, and falsified the sage book, because, having a wife and mother to maintain, he found his wages insufficient to cover his expenses. He neither drank nor gambled, but the lanchester Stipendiary on Wednesday sent him to gaol for six months.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday. Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one. We cannot under ordinary circumstances undertake return the manuscripts which are not used.

The License Compensation Question.

Sir,—ln reply to the letter of 11.1 r. H. .T. Longton in the " Warrington Guardian" of Wednesday he appears almost surprised that I shouldaccord to licensees a full recognition of the efforts they make in the direction of moderation and sobriety. Surely those who see most of the ruin to health, character, homes, and honour, wrought by intemperance, should be the most strenuous in their efforts to abate this evil.

Apart altogether from the moral aspect of the ques- tion it is dead against the interests of those who retail intoxicants to encourage intoxication. Should intemperance increase, more stringent would be the restrictions placed upon the sale of liquor. Let moderation prevail and intemperance cease then the restrictions already existing could be dispensed with; this all classes would hail with satisfaction, and it is perfectly natural that those engaged in the trade should do all they can to help this forward. I entirely agree with Mr. Longton that public houses supply a necessary want, that they are clubs to which working men Call resort for social intercourse and amusement. _

I think I agree with the following sentence, but I wish the writer had taken time to make its rneaninga little more clear " For these (work people) there is nowhere cheaper or better suited than a clean respectable well lighted inn, and I entirely dispute a necessary connection with either drunkenness, police charges, prison or asylum." If Mr. Longton means when he speaks of "necessary connection " that drinking should not lead to drunkenness then lam with him. but if he contends that drunkenness is not arrived at through drinking (be the inn ever so respectable and well lighted) that drunkenness does not entail police charges as well as a considerable proportion of prison and asylum costs then it seems to me that Mr. Long- ton approaches this aspect of the question with his eyes and ears closed.

lam glad to acknowledge Mr. Longton's correction on one point. I was not aware that licensees paid a yearly tax. Mr. Longton tells us this is about a year, but how does this effect the main question of Com- pensation ? It is in no sense the purchase of a perpetual right,but simply a tax for the right to sell for one year. It no more confers the right to compensation than in the case, say, of my carriage being damagetl, for which I pay a tax of a guinea a year. I could not claim compensation at the end of a year for the yearly tax I pay. I doubt not that Mr. Longton may seize upon this illustration and say granted about the tax, but what about the valu, of the carriage? I reply that the case is similar to the of the slaves, named by Mr. Robert Pierpoint. I paid for . the carriage as the slave-owner paid for his slaves, and , this would be the basis of the compensation claim. The original licensee paid nothing for his license apart from his annual tax, why then should the community pay when it has received nothing? But, says the corres- pondent, what -about the case, when a licensee has paid , another licensee a considerable sum and got his licence transferred. I would reply that if a buyer paid a large 1r sum for this, he has done it fully aware that there is the possibility of the licence not being renewed and he takes the chances which are known to be attached to such a purchase. Suppose Mr. Longton buys a business premises, he knows that one of the risks that such premises are sub- ject to, is destruction by fire, but he does not insure on the ground that, say for fifty years no fire has occurred. In case of loss would he have a right to consider him- self badly used because no one compensated him? In like manner if a publican is so careless as not to insure against the possibility of his license not being renewed; in case of non-renewal, while he may expect sympathy, he has no legal or moral claim to compensation. I see that Mr. Longton's clients insure their risks. This not only shows their good sense, but also their want of con:. fidence in the strength of their cleirn against the rate- payers. In respect to the increase Mr. Longton speaks of in the insurance rates, this is perfectly natural, the ,ICripplegate fires put up the insurance rates in that quarter 100 per cent. If the rates of existing Com- panies are graded to the risk it is well to pay them, if they are too high then is the opportunity for other Com- panies cutting in. A few years back a new Tanners Tariff came out, the rates of which were in the opinion of many in our trade excessive. We immediately formed a Leather Trade Insurance Company, which action at once brought about a reduction in the pro- posed tariff rates. A word or two about vested interest in a license. Mr. Longton says :—" And from the legal aspect beyond the case referred to (Sharpe v. Wakefield) the authority is all the other way. I say there is a property and an interest in a license." Ido not know if Mr. Longton lconsidershimself one of the authorities, but this sum- mary way of settling the question in dispute by a bald assertion of personal opinion, is somewhat amusing. The case referred to was recognised as a test case by the public, and by the trade, and was carried by the latter to the highest court in the hope of a reversal of the decision of the lower courts which was in efiect that there was no property in a license beyond a year, and that the magistrates had full power to decline, or to renew. If this decision was not final, if there was any chance of upsetting it, does anyone think that the trade would not have tried long ago ? But Mr. Langton is entirely wrong when he states that with the exception of this decision the authority is all the other way. I trust he will not think me presumptuous in venturing to correct him on a question of legal authorities but I would call kis attention to the following : Mr. Justice Field in the Court of Queen's Bench, November 1882, said : " The legislature recognises no vested right at all in any holder of a license. It does not treat the interest as a vested one in any way. Baron Pollock also in the Court of Queen's Bench, January 31, 1884 said : "The notion that there is a property of a landlord in a license cannot be considered as sound law." The late Mr. Nash, Barrister-at-Law, and counsel to the Licensed Victuallers Association, said:

Now, I am sorry to say having looked into this question most exhaustively, and having compared notes with my brethren well versed in these matters, that there cannot be the smallest doubt that. in the strict sense no. such thing as a vested interest exists vested interest should be avoided, as it infuriates every court from the Queen's Bench downwards. Mr. Longton can have more of these authorities if he likes but let these for the present suffice. On this point Mr. Longton has not a leg to stand upon. Nor does his comparison of the license of solicitors help his case. A solicitor is licensed solely on consideration of his fitness f(this duties and the evidence of this is the certificate of the Incorporated Law Society. He can only be removed from the roll if he proves unworthy of being on, whiles the principal consideration in the granting of a licen is the requirements of the locality. Mr. Longton must knew the main point that applicant's counsel endeavours to prove to the magistrates is that the inhabitants of the locality desire and require the license. Some people think there are too many solicitors. Were lin the habit of calling names I should adopt the term Mr. Longton applied to some teetotalars, and say that such were bigoted, but were the number of solicitors ten times what it is, those who grant the licenses for solicitors would have no legal right to deny it license to an additional one presenting proper credentials. In the eyes of the low the two cases stand on an entirely different footing. It is very necessary that before any new acknowledge- ment of a vested interest is made the claim should be rigourously examined, and if it is faulty resisted. If the ratepayers are indifferent and allow this question to be rushed, and a new vested interest established they will one day wake up to find what a load they have al- lowed to be hung round their necks. With the vested interests already established and new importunate claimants demanding recognition and payment out of rates!and taxes the country runs the risk of being seri- ously impoverished and social progress arrested by the fear of expense.

CHARLES E. PARKER

Penketh May 22nd, 1903. ' Mr. Monks and the Education Committee.

Sirr—Your contemporary,the ."Warringt on Guardian" last week commented upon the work of the Education Committee and upon the work of the old Technical Tn. struction Committee in the following language :—" the dying Committee been less ambitious and had had the sense to leave the Education Committee fre

appoint its own officers when the proper time came, all this friction would have been saved."

T say nothing of the discourtesy of such language. T will confine myself to the actual facts. When the New Technicol Buildings, were being erected, the Committee deferred the re-organization of the work until the building was re.ady, as their experience had shown that it would be better to sever the work and make it in- dependent of the organization of the Museum and Library Committee. Circumstances in connection with the Pupil Teachers' centre made it possible for the Committee to incorpor- ate this work with their own. Acting upon the advice of the Technical Sub-Committee, who had fully con- sidered the whole matter with a fuller knowledge of all the conditions than anyone else, it was decided to appoint a Director of Technical and Secondary Educa- tion, who should also take up the work of the Pupil Teachers' Centre. One result of this arrangement was, the committee found themselves in a position to offer a better salary than they would have felt justified in doing for Technical and Secondary Education only. They accordingly advertised for a director at a salary

===== 0002601_19030529_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Friday, May 29, 1903. A WARRINGTON SOLICITOR AND HIS NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. CLIENT.

of £3OO a year,and made an appointment that those best I qualified to judge consider an excellent one in the interests of the education of the town. Nothing what- ever was said about Elethentary Education, as the Com- mittee did not feel justified in anticipating any appoint- ment for Elementary Education. They confined them- selves to their own business. Let me say in this connection the necessity for the appointment was urgent in consequence of the com- plaints of the Pupil Teachers' Committee of the delay in making suitable provision for the work taken over from them. There was no secrecy as all these arrange- ments were submitted to the Town Council and con- firmed by them. Almost the first thing the New Educa- tion Committee did was to appoint Mr. Murray secre- tary, and to make him director of elementary education also, all this in addition to the work he was appointed to do at a salary of £3OO a year. Now we come to the work of the New Committee and your contemporary says" Even assuming that the special sub-committee had been properly constituted and authorised to do what Mr. Monks said they were authorised to do, that would not cover the appointment of the staff and the raising of the director's salary." The special sub-committee have made no appoint- ment, neither have they raised the salary of the Director. They were authorized to make recommenda- tions, and they accordingly considered the whole question of what was required and what would be a fairremuner- ation for the work. They unanimously concluded to recommend the appointment of a staff of six, a chief clerk, a stores clerk, a book-keeper and minute clerk, and three minor clerks, and the salaries to be paid to each.4ltlwas not unreasonable for Mr. Murray to expect some remuneration for the additional work thrownupon him, and the special sub-committee recommended in his case that :£lOO a year with .E 25 a year advance no to £5OO a year was reasonable, and such as they believed would be satisfactory to the committee. The committee have approved the recommendation for the staff but not for the Director's salary. The old Technical Committee have handed over their work with an efficient staff, and with an arrangement that has enabled them to easily pay a salary of £3OO a year to their Director. The new committee are so satis- fied with the work that they have taken it over with the staff and now ask the Director to take over the responsi- bility of efficiently organizing the work of Elementary Education with its 21 schools and 13,596 scholars for the sum of £25 a year with an advance of £25 ayear up to £5OO. I leave such a proposition without com- ment for the serious consideration of those interested in the education of the town. The results have proved that the Technical Sub-committee was right, and the town is now profiting by their good sense in suggesting an arrangement which is both economical and to the advantage of education. Yet your contemporary ignores all this and bluntly accuses them of secretly plotting ambitious schemes and practically tells them their work has been badly done. Your contemporary also poses as a defender of right, and yet without suffi- cient knowledge of the facts makes accusations that are false, does the director of education a grave in- justice and does its best to discredit a body of men who have done good, honest work for the past 12 years in the interests of the education of the to wn.—Yo urs truly, • F. MONKS.

Walton Old Hall, 28th May, 1903. , WARRINGTON TRAINING COLLEGE. The Musical Examination.

Dr. Arthur Somervell visited the Warrington Train- ing College on Tuesday for the purpose of conducting the annual musical examination. As usual the collec- tive test took the form of a concert in the evening, and by the invitation of the Principal—the Rev. Morley Stevenson--manv ladies and gentlemen interested in the work of the institution attended. The works rendered were Mendelssohn's "Hymn of Praise " and '• Ninety- fifth Psalm," and the performance throughout gave the greatest pleasure to the audience. About 130 students assisted in the choruses, and the male voices were selec- ted front local choirs. The principal vocalists were Miss Ellen Atkinson. Miss Constance Pople, Miss Ivy Camden, Miss Charlotte Quinn and Mr. W. J. Thomas. Mr. W. S. Woods once more wielded the baton and under his able guidance both compositionswere very carefully interpreted. The choruses were given with, marked precision and expression, and the soloists were on the whole heard to advantage. Valuable assistance was rendered by an orchestra composed as follows :—Mr. R. H. Pearson (first violin), Mr. E. H. Clare (second. violin), Mr. G. Johnson (viola), Mr. H. Bond (violon- cello), Mr. R. Whitfield (double bass), Miss Hackett (piano), and Mr. W. A. Wood (harmonium). At the close the Rev. MORLEY STEVENSON briefly addressed the assembly. He remarked that he did not know whether the visitors were aware that they had been assisting at one of those functions which they delighted to make pleasant in the age in which they lived and which was commonly called an examination. (Laughter.) The singing to which they had been listening was part of the practical musical examina- tion of the students,, and it should therefore be borne in mind that the chorus was not a picked body of voices such as they would hear at an ordinary concert but comprised the whole of the students. The lady soloists were also students at the college. Con- tinuing, the Principal said that musical training was a part of their curriculum, but they are not able, much as they might wish, to devote more time to music than, say,to arithmetic. (Laughter.) Indeed, he was afraid not so much. Therefore, the audience must not think that they spent their time entirely in such pursuits as that. Would that they could do so. (Hear, hear and laughter.) Music occupied the same position on their time table that other subjects did. He would ask the examiner to make some remarks. Dr. SOMERVELL said he would like in his own name and he was sure in the name of the audience to thank the students very much for the delightful performances they had given, and also the soloists and conductor for their services. The speaker alluded to the subject of musical training in schools and emphasised the import- ance of teaching children to read music. Enormous progress might be made if only ten minutes every day were devoted to the subject. He asked the students to be very careful to teach good music and said he had been very much struck with the poor quality of the music which was sung in the schools. He considered that the best music which could be taught—and music of which the children and teachers would never tire— was to be found in the national songs of England, Scot- land and Ireland. He did not mean the patriotic airs only, but what were sometimes called the folk songs of the country. Dr. Somervell dealt with some of the objections raised in a blue book to the teaching of old love, hunting, and sea songs, and said he thought they might put all the reasons on one side. Let them teach the children something which they would always like to know and which teachers would always like to teach. Every child ought to know as many of the national songs of the country as possible. just as it was necessary to know parts of the Bible. If they gave their children nothing but the best music they would be doing a great work. (Applause.)

DOUBLE FUNERAL AT WARRINGTON. The Late Messrs. Mottram and Whitehouse.

Amid manifestations of general regret the funerals of the late Mr, George Whitehouse confectioner, Bridge- street, Warrington and his father-in-law, Mr. James Mottram, The Ackers, Latchford, took place at the War- rington Cemetery at noon on Sunday. Sequel to an Action for Slander.

Mr. Arthur Browne, solicitor, was the plaintiff in an action heard at the Warrington County Court on Thurs- day, before his Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C. He claimed £ll 18s. 7d. from Michael O'Brien employed at Messrs. Walker's brewery, being the balance of an. I account due for .professional services rendered and out lof pocket expenses relative to an action for slander and false imprisonment against a man named Berry. The case was heard at Preston and Mr. Browne attended before Mr. Wilson, acting under sheriff. The jury assessed the dam igee at 403. and judgment was entered for O'Brien. The costs amounted to £2l is. 7d. O'Brien had paid £9 3s. on account, leaving £ll 18s. 7d., the' amount now claimed. On entering the box, defendant behaved in an I eccentric manner, and remarked that he hoped the Judge would allow him to tell the truth, and nothing but the truth. The Judge: You must not talk so much, if you please; wait for your turn. Mr. Crompton, in opening the case, said that in April, 1900, the defendant was arrested as a pickpocket at the • instigation of a man named Berry. He was taken to the Police Station, and on it being found that he was not a pickpocket he was released. For the satisfaction I of his employer, O'Brien took an action against the man who had been the cause of his arrest. He only wanted nominal damages the main object being to clear himself of any suspicion of being a pickpocket. The action was brought but the man Berry did not enter an appear- ance and judgement went by default. Mr. Browne had then to appear before the Sheriff's jury for the purpose of assessing damages, and judgement was entered for O'Brien for 40s and costs. The costs were taxed and amounted to £2l. The defendent in the action ran away and O'Brien refused to pay the costs. His Honour : That is a natural disposition of people. (Laughter.) Arthur Roberts, in the employ of Mr. Arthur Browne, proved to delivering a bill to the defendant. Arthur Browne,-the plaintiff, stated in evidence that the costs were taxed between party and party at £2l. He had not charged the defendant anything like the amount he could have done. O'Brien had paid him £9 3s. before he sent in his bill. He happened to make use of an expression before the Sheriff's jury which dis- pleased the defendant. His Honour would not consider the expression a wrong one if O'Brien carried on as he had done. The defendant, however, when he got out- side told him he would not pay. HisHonour : It is a sort of deduction for bad language. (Laughter.) The Defendant: I only came here for honestyand for justice. I did not come here for law. (Laughter.) I am willing to pay him if he is out of pocket. His Honour: Apart from your desire to get justice generally, why don't you pay this amount? The Defendant: • I cannot do it. Are you aware I am only a 23-shillings-a-week man." ' His Honour: What is your defence? The Defendent : They told me at Preston that I was going to get 40s. His Honour: You have not got your 40s. The Defendant; Nor never will get it (Laughter). I will cross-examine Mr. Browne: Does he know where the man Berry is? (Laughter). Mr. A. Browne said he had been. unable to trace Berry. ' The Defendant :' Have younot been the legal adviser of Berry's wife and family? Mr. Browne: I never knew the man. . The Defendant: Were you not the legal adviser in a case between Berry and his wife? His Honour: lam always prepared to give every person an opportunity of stating his case, but a quarrel between the defendant Berry and his wife maybe an interesting subject to consider, but it has nothing to do with this case. Mr. Browne said he had been unable to ascertain the whereabouts of Berry. The Defendant : He is in Hollinwood, near Man- chester. His Honour: You are responsible for the payment of this bill. The Defendant: You are clever if you get any out of me. Mr. Browne: He is a man of means. The Defendant elected to be sworn and give evidence. On being sworn by the usher he said: I have sworn on the English version, which I believe is the same as Douay. If Mr. Browne will be lenient with me I will pay it. lam not going to be a preacher and a teacher, and I thank your honour. The Judge entered judgment for the plaintiff, the amount to be -paid in a month. Defendant,: I thank yourhonour very much for your patience in hearing us.

MEETING OF HAIRDRESSERS. No More Penny Shaves.

For some considerable time the question of an increase in the price of shaving has been under discussion by the Warrington and District Hairdressers' Association. Meetings have been held and visitations made to all in the trade which culminated in a meeting being held last Tuesday evening in the Cocoa rooms,Horsemarket street under the presidency of Mr. A. Lamb. Mr. Priestnall president of the Widnes Hairdressers' Society, and Mr. Mercer secretary of the St Helens Society,were present and gave addresses on the question of the increased price. The former gentlemen gave the experience of Widnes and pointed out the value of unity and the ad- vantages accruing therefrom. He said the people of Widnes had proved most friendly to them and had helped them by their support. Mr. Mercer speaking at some length corroborated Mr. Priestnall'sremarks.He pointed , out that Warrington was affiliated with the Northern Con n tiestlairdressers Federation who had set a minimum of 10 for the price of shaving. All the Branches of the Federation were advancing in ' the matter Among others who had established the increased price were: Liverpool and district, Birkenhead and district, Widnes, St. Helens, Knutsford, Northwich, Leigh and Bedford, Tyldsley, Atherton, Eccles, Pendlebnry and Swinton, Manchester and district, Salford, Ashton- under-Lyne, Hyde, Stalybridge, Dukinfleld, Bury, , Bolton, Rochdale, Middleton, Halifax, Ste., Scc., in fact from Durham in the north-east to Bristol in the south- west, right through the country there was a general I rise. And it was needed, for owing to competition many I members of the trade were not getting a living wage added to which, expenses of conducting a business had increased and were increasing. They had found no di fn- . culty in St. Helens with their customers who on having the case put fairly before th:ln had approved its just- ness. What had been done in so many places could be I done in Warrington which town was identically in the same position as others. Mr. PODMORE, sen., supported the speakers, and gave interesting comparisons in wages paid in other trades now and 50 years ago. All trades had increased their wages—in some eases—almost double, yet the price of shaving was still a penny. He claimed justification in I the advance of the price to Ltd. He had fullest confi- dence that the people wouldsupport them in the matter. Mr. HEDGCOCK, senr., Mr. M'CLEw,- Mr. OLDFIELD, Mi. MAILINGS, Mr. DERDYSEIDIF.and others also spoke. —Ultimately it was resolved that on and after June Bth, 1903, the minimum price of shaving be lid.—Votes •of thanks to Mr. Priestnall and Mr. Mercer for their attendance and addresses closed the meeting.

CHESHIRE EDUCATION PROPOSALS. School Board Officials and Teachers Retained.

The first meeting of the Cheshire County .Education Committee under the new Act was held at Crewe on Wednesday. Col. Dixon (chairman of the County Council) proposed the election as the .chairman of the committee of Mr. C. E. Thornycroft, and the motiOn was carried unanimously, as was also one to appoint Dr. Hodgson as deputy chairman. • Dr. -Hewitt asked that Winnington and the parishes grouped with it should be allowed to retain their con- nection with Northwich, and be covered by the same committee. He pointed out that inasmuch as Messrs. Brunner.Mond,and Co. had voluntarily rated themselves. for many years to carry on technical education in that district, they were entitled to this consideration. After some discussion a sub-committee was appointed to con- sider the advisability of altering and adjusting the educational areas of Northwich, Bucklow, and Wirral. Colonel Thorpe, referring to the proposal to . appoint seven members upon the sub-committee for each of the boroughs of Macclesfield, Congleton, Stalybridge,Crewe, Hyde, Dukinfield, and the Wallasey Urban District for the purposes of higher education, said that he thought the local authorities should be asked to select their eight members first. This suggestion was adopted. After considerable discussion a motion was adopted to appoint a sub-committee for each union area to send in names to the committee from whom a selection may be made of persons suitable to form the administrative sub -committee. Sub-committees for agricultural edu- cation and sub-committees for other special forms of higher education were appointed. As to the existing school officials, 8•:.c., the Clerk was directed to communicate with the various clerks to the educational committees in the county area, asking themn to retain their office, the question of salaries to be con- sidered later. It was,decided to ask the school attend- ance officers to hold their respective positions at the same salaries.

With regard to the teaching staffs of the various schools. it was decided that their appointments should be continued, and that the salaries should be revised, if necessary, later, after the committee had had certain proposed agreements suggested by the teachers' repre- sentative under consideration.

Cricket.

Warrington are doing great things this season. Not a single defeat has yet been experienced and on Saturday the St. Helens eleven visited the Arpley enclosure and received ,an unmerciful drubbing. Although the excellent condition of the wicket favoured the batsmen the Warrington bowlers did their work so thoroughly that the St. Helens men had all to retire in three quarters of an hour for the insignificant total of 24. The Warringtonians were in splendid form and amassed 100 runs, thus running out victors by a! margin of 76. For once the Weather , was all that could be desired and there was a large gathering of spectators.

St. Helens are not considered a formidable eleven. and their display on Saturday was extremely weak. The credit of Warrington's performance must not, however, be under-estimated. Lynes and J. W.Bibby bowled in most effective style and their deliveries gave the visitors continual trouble. Lynes captured 6 wickets for 5 runs and Bibby was responsible for 3 wickets for 19 runs. S. G-. Winston was the only St. Helens batsman who attained double figures, his score being 12.

. • 'Warrington had no difficulty. in "passin, the St. Helens aggregate and when that last wicket fell the century had just been reached. J. A. Briggs wielded the willow in fine style, his innings being productive of 19 not out). J. W. Bibby batted Well for his 16, whilst W. H. Podinore and Lvnes were each respons- ible for 14. The St. Helens bowlers failed to attain any striking averages.

The second eleven are to be congratulated upon their decisive victory over St. Helens second. They fared even better than the senior contingent the majority in their favour being no smaller than 143. D. Plinston (40), W. Richardson (39), A. J. Hallam (28), T. Wright (28), and E. E. Woods (22) rendered valu- able service with the bat, .whilst W. Tickle ( 23) was the top scorer for St. Helens. Woods • and Wright bowled splendidly fir the victors, Wright taking three wickets for two runs and Woods three for seven.

Sankey Wire Mills had to admit defeat at the hands of Hough Green, as they compiled only 31 as against their opponents' 85. Laws was the highest scorer for the winners, knockineup 30. Not a single Wire Mills representatiye reached double figures.

Warrington Grammar School eleven entertained a team from Hulme Grammar school, but the visitors proved too strong for them. . The home team were all dismissed for 35 and in response the visitors had compiled 43 for the loss of only 4 wickets when play ceased. Swindells did the best work with the bat, his efforts realizing 24 (not out);

Cheshire tines Engineers gained a victory at the expense of Latchford Baptists, the aggregates being 84 and 55 respectively. P. Lawless -(24), and Jr'. Darg,an (18), were the highest scorers for the Engineers, A. Albinson and J. Lester knocking up 10 each for the Baptists.. Dargan's bowling analysis was as follows :-- 5 avers, 3 maidens, 4 runs, 0 wickets. •

Fairfield were at Irme on Saturday last no a team, captained by a late member of their club (Mr. W. Travis) from Liverpool. The visiting team batted first, and their first two batsmen, defying all the opposing bowlers, made the score of 46 before being separate-d.. After their wickets fell, however, the best of the team only compiled ten runs between them. Fairfield had the hardest of luck ; 4of their batsmen being lbw. Their total score only realised 31, thus reing beaten by 26. To-day Fairfield again meet a Liverpool team, viz., St. 'Crypians, on their own ground.

Sankey Wire Mills 2nd had matters practically . all their own way in the engagement with St. Albans. The latter were dismissed after compiling 41, of which A. Stirrup was responsible for 24 (not out). The Wire Mills got to work in splendid form and after knocking. up 109 for the loss of 3 wickets declared their innings. Kenwright was the mainstay of the winners, his inninffs being productive of 31. J. Booth also batted wellfor his 27. A. Stirrup was in good form for St. Alban's, and knocked up 24 (not out.)

Penketh entertained Crosfield's, and had the satis- faction of achieving victory by a margin of 22. The respective totals were 42 and 20 and not a single batsmen attained double figures.

Penketh 2nd did not emulate the example of the senior contingent but sustained defeat at the hands of Bewsey-road Wesleyans. Their combined efforts pro- duced 26, whereas the Wesleyans piled on 51. A. Jolley deserves much of the credit for the victory, for he scored :31 and captured 8 wickets for. 9 runs. E. Davies (10) was the highest scorer for the opposing side.

Cycling. W. Collin of Warrington, has again entered the. rac- ing track, after a long suspension, and on Saturday he won the first prize in the half mile cycle race at Nelson Cricket Club sprts. He also carried off the second prize in the two miles handicap. C. E. Monk, of, War- rmgton, also gained honours on Saturday. He secured the first prize in the one mile handicap at Bridle Subscription Band sports.

The St. Helens Cyclists' Club carried'ont their fifty miles road cxupetitioa in Cheshire on Thursday ,last, being favoured with ideal weather. Only four members turned out, and two of the most promising had the misfortune to break down. 11.. F. Honiton started out to lower the club record of 2hrs. 41min., but had the misfortune to meet with a serious puncture at Holmes Chapel whim he had .r,otte about thirty miles and had pt the wind behind him for the run home. J. Peel, of Peasley Cross, punctured early on, and afterwards broke a chain.. He had no fewer than four mounts, but suffered so much delay at Oongleton that he gave up. R. Lawson went through in 2hrs. 50min. 45sec., just under a minute outside first-class time. F. Appleton rode the fifty miles in 3hrs. 17min.

Local enthusiasts who desire to combine an indul gence in the pastime with the acquisition of military tactical knowledge have of late been participating with zest in the work of the Volunteer Cycling Corps. Taking advantage of the glorious weather Saturday last was devoted to some interesting operations in Cheshire. The general idea was that a hostile force ' with headquarters at Runcorn had captured some scouts belonging to a Warri,ngton force and the men were being convoyed under a sufficient escort to Great Budworth. The escort which was under the com- mand of Colour-Sergeant Jones on leaving the Drill Hall proceeded through Padgate, Warburton and Lymm to High Legh Hall and then struck 'across the Knutsford-road to‘viirds Great Budworth. Another , company of cyclists bent on attacking the Runcorn convoy and rescuing the prisoners left Warrington under the command of Sergeants Holloway and Owens and journeyed through Stretton to within about a mile of Great Budworth. Here the force held posses- sion of three crossl roads and scouts were sent out to see if the enemy could be. sighted. They soon came into touch with the convoy and of course the main , company was quickly placed on the alert.A.boutan hour' skirmishing ensued between the opposing forces ands at half past six, Captain Crosfield, who had been ant lig as. umpire, blew the whistle for the cessa- tion of hostilities. He gave his decision in favour of Sergeants Holloway and .Owens' company which,he said, would 'have, succeeded in rescuing the prisoners. He added .that had Colour-Sergeant Jones sent out a flanking movement earlier the opposing side would have been attacked in the rear and beaten. As it was, a flanking force under Corporal Lowe was sent out, but the move was made too late, the whistle sounding as soon as the men opened fire.

A civilian cyclist who came upon the scene about the commenc,ment of hostilities evidently thought he had landed in a dangerous quarter. When the convoy sighted the attacking company, the older was given for the men to dismount and seek cover: The cyclists at once lay full length in Some long grass, but the other side had opened lire upon them. Hearing the order and apparently concluding that the rifles were charged with bullets the civilian thought it prudent to follow the example of the volunteers and he also dismounted and lay patiently in the grass. The volunteers could scarcely repress a smile when shortly afterwards he inquired if it was now safe to proceed. Of course he was at once informed that there had been no danger whatever and pursued his Ivay probably feeling sur- prised that matters should be taken -so much in earnest in mimic warfare. Of course the object o these manreuvres is to .train the participants so thatf they would be prepared if ever necessity arose to assist in a real conflict.

Angling. The Warrington Workingmen's Anglers' Associati?o. have just secured through the Runcorn Anglers' Assoc Lir tion•fishing rights in the Duke's canal. The length IS. from the wooden bridge at Preston Brook to Runcorn, which is considered by many anglers to be about the best length in the canal. The terms offered by the Runcorn Association were accepted by the committee last week,and were so favourable that it was decided not to increase the spbscription, although a large number of valuable prizes are to be given by the associattc:a during the season. Owing to some of the waters in which three of the association's matches were to be fished having been taken up, a meeting is to be held to decide upon fresh places in which to hold them. The Northern Anglers' match, which was originally selected to be fished in the Duke's canal at Warrington, is to be held at Waverton on the Saturday,,before Walking Day.

The secretary of the Worsley Canal Fishery Board (Mr. G. Smeeth, 61 Hanging Ditch, Manchester)writes to say that from to-day the -Bridgewater Canal under their control is open for the season. The season tickets are ss. which cover all occasions. Day tickets are is and for matches, 3d, for time limit of match, fer non season ticket holders. Ten days' notice of matches is required and permits and licences can be obtained from W. P. Knight 26, Hewitt street, Latch fort and Mr. Fred J. Emson, Granpenhall. No Sun- day matches are allowed. Those members of local clubs who have not arranged for the fishing in the Canal are requested to communicate with either

Messrs Knight of Emson. During the week the River Bollin has been in excellent order for angling operations and since the warm weather has been in evidence, several nice evening rises of trout have been observed. Messrs. Baker, Webster, Nixon, Green, Higginbottom, Brown,. Waywell, Robinson, Shepherd, Milner, Bell and ',ell° have all done fairly well. Mr. Brown of Manchester registered thirteen sizeable trout—a good record for any river. Mr. Webster had one well over a pound, but Mr., J. Waywell heads the list with a well- conditioned trout of slightly over two pounds.

PEN KETH. PARISH COUNCIL. A special meeting of the Parish Council was held in the Wesleyan Schools on Monday evening. There were present—Dr. Murray (chairman), Messrs. C. E. Parker, .1. J. Withinshaw, E. W. Clarke, J. S. Snoddy, and E. Forster (clerk.) Mr. John Midwood, the newly-appointed member„ signed the usual declaration and took his seat ' on the Council. FOOTPATH DIVERSION—LIVERPOOL-ROAD TO BACK-LANE. The Clerk stated that he had received a letter from the Great Sankey Parish Council in reply to the one sent to them respecting the footpath across Mr. Thomas OWen's field, and they seemed to think that it was a matter for the landlord to deal with. The Chairman said that Mr. Owen had now abandoned his intention of diverting the footpath as:previously dis- cussed, and the matter ended. TELEPHONE WIRES. Correspondence between the Council and the Tele- phone Company was read. relating to the wires crossing the recreation ground, and permission was asked by the company to erect a pole at the bottom end of the ground. On the proposition of Mr. Parker, seconded ,by Mr. Withinshaw, the request was granted subject to an annual charge. DUST CONTRACTOR RESIGNS. The Chairman stated that the dust contractor had sent in his resignation, and would in the ordinary course of events relinquish his office on the 17th of June. After half an hour's discussion it was agreed that thp Rural District Council be askedto advertise the vacancy and solicit tenders. COMPLAINT ABOUT THE GAS. Mr. Midwood presented a petition complaining of the low pressure of gas in the village, and asking the Council to take the matter up strenuously with the Warrington Corporation Gas Works and have it attended to at once. The petition was signed by nearly all the gas consumers in the township. Mr. Parker stated that it was not so much the low pressure as the irregularity. On the proposition of Mr. Midwood, seconded by Mr Parker, it was decided to send the petition to the Gas Works with a letter from the Council endorsing the statements and pressing for an improvement. PADGATE. THE NEWCHURCH FIRE.—On Sunday, at Christ Church, special offertories were devoted to the rebuild- ing fund of Newchurch Church, which was recently destroyed by fire. The morning preacher was the Vicar who gave a brief history of the church and an interest- ing account of Bishop Wilson, of Sodor and Man, who, for some time, was curate of Wiuwick in charge of Newchurch., The evening preacher was the Rev. E. W. W. Kaye,'.rector of Newchurch, who announced that already over £1,700 had been received or promised towards the rebuilding fund. The offertories amounted to £9. The 1 North American Animated Photo ' Company. As will be seen from our advertising columns the above is to visit Parr Hall, Warrington next week when the exhibition recently given in the SC George' Hall, Liverpool, will be reproduced at the Parr Hall The company stands unrivalled in the excellence o its entertainment. They will show pictures of life in Canada, defeat of British troops by the mad Mullah and hundreds of other interesting scenes. The visit should prove a very popular one.

RUNCORN NOTES. [BY A CORRESPONDENT]. It is stated that an inquiry will be held during the latter part of next month by the County Council into the Urban Council's absorption scheme. Counsel are being engaged by the Urban Council and also by the Rural Council who are opposing the scheme. At time of writing itPuts not transpired whomthe County Council have appointed to act on their behalf. • • ----- The Runcorn Football:Club have improved their finan- cial position compared with theseason last year. They are by no means completely out of the wood but they wouldhave been had they been fortunate enough to have survived the fourth round of the cup-ties. As tifetreasurer plaintively remarked it would have set them on their legs." • This year Mr. Ellison, secretary, was opposed by Mr. Abel, assistant secretary. This fact in conjunction with rumours and charges of" cliqueism "against certain members of the Committee, all, it was alleged, intended to harass the secretary, made the members roll up to the annual meeting in unusual numbers. By an overwhelming vote of practically two to one, Mr. Ellisons services were retained amid uproarious applause. The election of the Committee also gave rise to ex- citement. Several of the old committee were not re- elected and when the results were known there were cheers and counter cheers. The gentlemennominated who were not elected were Messrs W. Brimelow, T. Cooke,H. Wright, J. Green, G. Jones, T. Robinson. On Tuesday afternoon a local journalist, Mr. W. J. Molyneux, who has worked the Runcorn district for some years, was presented with a handsome watch by his Press colleagues on his departure for the United States.—Mr. F. Whalley. in making the presentation, eulogised the work of Mr. Molyneux, and asked him to accept the watch as a small token of esteem.—Mr. Arolsrneux suitably acknowledged the gift a vote of thanks afterwards being passed to Mr. F. Fennelly for having occupied the chair.
